| `@X145` | ⚠⚠ **the FENCE RELAXES on the INSTANCE and NOT on the RULES** (refined 2026-08-26) — `@X118` is strict in-mission because the instance is live and the player still has to decide; **once the base is finished there is no open question left to short-circuit**, so what happened may be said freely.  ⚠⚠ **But what to do DIFFERENTLY stays the player's to derive** — that is the transferable axis and it must be earned, so *"the tower was black from the third wave and nobody came"* passes while *"you should build tighter perimeters"* does not.  ⚠ `@X122`'s rules-vs-instance distinction, one layer up.  ⚠ The accounts stay PARTIAL and may disagree, so it does not become an oracle | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f |
| `@X146` | ⚠⚠ **the roster is a SENSOR LOADOUT** — a sortie with no scout comes back with nobody who can say where the wave came from, so `@X125`'s template choice is **also a knowledge decision** and not only a labour one.  ⚠⚠ **And there is an empty chair**: a helper left behind is not there to be debriefed, so `DESIGN.md` § 14's *"cost of haste"* reaches into the station and takes an account away — `@X132`'s ledger producing consequence at zero design cost.  ⚠ Two refusals: **not a scoring screen** (§ 14 has no fail screen; a grade announces what the design says must be *felt across the sequence*) and **not an interrogation UI** (no dialogue tree; skippable in one action for `@X120`'s returning player) | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f |
| `@X147` | ⚠⚠ **the crew are UNRELIABLE NARRATORS of the mission** (owner, 2026-08-26) — and it is what makes `@X145`'s relaxed fence safe: a debrief that relaxed the fence AND spoke with authority would hand over the dominant axis, so unreliability is the safety valve that forces the player to **triangulate**.  ⚠ Four sources, none of them lying: **position** (they saw their part), **expertise** (a miner reads a collapse as bad rock, a security officer reads it as a breach), **stake** (somebody left outside the wall remembers differently) and **salience** (what nearly killed them looms over what decided the base).  ⚠⚠ **It is LEARNABLE, which makes it a progression** — the crew's biases are stable RULES and the sortie is the instance (`@X122`), so calibrating your own people accumulates on `@X117` and nobody had to author it.  ⚠ Mechanically free: an account is limited to hexes the helper occupied, which the simulation already knows | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f |
| `@X148` | ⚠⚠ **NOTHING THEY SAY IS FALSE — the skew is SELECTION, EMPHASIS and OMISSION** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"they will not state a wrong thing, they will just skew to their own perspective"*).  ⚠ *"Nobody came for that tower"* is TRUE; the skew is offering it as **the** reason the base fell when the wall was also mis-sited where the speaker never went.  ⚠⚠ **So the player can trust every sentence and still not have the picture** — a debrief can never push somebody toward a worse decision by asserting something false; the worst case is an INCOMPLETE picture, which is the intended difficulty.  ⚠ It also makes the build trivially safe: **a filter over a true event list**, weighted by domain and by where the speaker was — no fiction generator, nothing that invents a claim.  ⚠ Two supporting rules: bias misleads in **degree never direction** (or players tune the crew out and `@X137`'s onboarding role collapses), and bias is **consistent per character** (one that varies run to run is indistinguishable from lying and cannot be learned).  ⚠ This REFINES `@X129` rather than reversing it: **arbitrary error is noise, systematic bias is character** | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2c, § P2f |
| `@X149` | ⚠⚠ **they REPORT, they do not PRESCRIBE** — the debrief's version of *points, never concludes*.  A crew member may say what happened and offer an opinion built out of true things they witnessed; they may not recommend the fix.  ⚠ The owner's own statement of the goal is the test: *"the player need their own judgement to piece together what should be done/enhanced the next run"* — **a crew member who recommends the fix has done the piecing together**, and the player's progression is what paid for it | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f |
| `@X150` | ⚠⚠ **THE CREW DO NOT KNOW MORE THAN THE PLAYER — they are on this planet for the first time too** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ Already the fiction: `SETTING.md` § The recruitment has Centaur Mining specialising in **automated asteroid mining** and taking a contract they have no experience of.  ⚠⚠ **The governing distinction is NOTICING vs KNOWING**: a past job gives them trained ATTENTION (a miner's eye goes to the ground) and never world knowledge (what Dryopea's ground actually does).  ⚠⚠ **So `@X129`'s fence needs no discipline to hold — they do not conclude because they CANNOT**, and a rule enforced by the world costs nothing to maintain where one enforced by restraint erodes the first time somebody writes a helpful line.  ⚠ They still onboard (`@X137`), by **drawing attention rather than explaining**: a lost player does not need an explanation, they need somewhere to look | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2c |
| `@X151` | ⚠⚠ **the crew LEARN ALONGSIDE the player, and it is audible progression** — arriving knowing nothing, they come to know what the player knows, so *"that ground looks soft to me"* becomes *"that's like the third base, the ground gave way there too"*.  ⚠ It is the crew's version of `@X117` and the one form of progression the player can HEAR.  ⚠⚠ **And it keeps them from ever overtaking the player**: they learn from the sorties the player ran, so their knowledge is a **subset** of what the player had access to — a crew member who knew something the player could not have learned is the failure `@X150` exists to prevent.  ⚠ Composes with `@X131`'s ledger (they may only refer to a base they were ON) and with `@X102`'s knowledge tree | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2c |
| `@X152` | ⚠⚠ **the crew are the WINDOW INTO THE BIGGER UNIVERSE** (owner, 2026-08-26) — *"the player gets pushed head-first into the scenario but the helpers have a background"*.  ⚠⚠ **It answers a hole nobody had named**: `SETTING.md` is ~2 300 lines and the game has **no delivery channel** for it — `@X137` refuses a tutorial, § HUD refuses an overlay, and `@X102`'s knowledge tree only covers what is found ON the planet.  ⚠⚠ **It does not contradict `@X150`**: the split is **Dryopea vs everywhere else** — novices about this planet, natives of the setting, so *the crew can explain where you all came from and not where you are*.  ⚠ It gives BACKGROUNDS a third job (starting skills, habits of attention, and now **a vantage on the universe**), which is why a hive dweller and a spacer are worth being different people | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2g |
| `@X153` | ⚠⚠ **FOUR channels, and they must not be collapsed into one dialogue system** — each has a different direction, trigger and cost: the **idle remark** (push, no task, what they notice here and now, free), **crew chatter** (push, ambient, overheard, the OUTER world, free), **ask them** (⚠ pull, in-mission, the player DRIVES to them and spends time — costing the clock and the position), and the **debrief** (pull, post-mission, their account of the sortie, free).  ⚠ *Ask them* passes `DESIGN.md` § What kind of game this is **outright**: it is a POSITION and not a keypress, the same shape as `plans/17` § T1's tower repair | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2g |
| `@X154` | ⚠⚠ **chatter YIELDS to the nudge** — `@X142`'s idle remark works because speech is RARE, so constant chatter turns a helper speaking into background noise and takes `@X137`'s onboarding role down with it.  ⚠ Chatter is sparse, must not repeat (a line heard twice is wallpaper), and while the player is stalled with something to point at, **the nudge gets the channel**.  Atmosphere is what plays when nothing needs saying | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2g |
| `@X155` | ⚠ **the crew carry truths 1 and 2; the PLANET keeps truth 3** — `SETTING.md` § The quarantine's three concentric truths divide cleanly: the public *haywire-AI* story and the military *nothing-leaves-without-a-permit* reality are the world the crew live in and may speak of (partially, with rumour attached), while the personality-altered AIs, the underground humans and the portal are found on the ground via `@X102` and are forbidden to the crew by `@X150`.  ⚠⚠ So the crew carry a great deal of setting **without touching the mystery** | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2g |
| `@X156` | ⚠⚠ **UI-LIGHT: presence IS the interaction, and there is NO LIST OF QUESTIONS ANYWHERE** (owner, 2026-08-26) — *"getting to them and interacting gives them a moment to comment but the player has no list of questions to ask of them"*.  ⚠ So *ask them* is not asking: it is **being there long enough that they say something**, which is `DESIGN.md` § 11 § Position triggers applied to conversation — *activated through motion, not by typing*.  ⚠⚠ **The player chooses WHO and WHEN; the crew choose WHAT** — which is `@X142`'s rule with the selector swapped, making push and pull **one mechanism with two doors**: the game picks the speaker when a helper is idle, the player picks by driving over, and the content is the speaker's either way.  ⚠ The line appears near them **in the world**, never in a panel; it is the same verb as repairing a tower.  ⚠ Applies at the station too (`@X146`) | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2g, § P2f |
| `@X157` | ⚠⚠ **the crew have real PERSONALITY — class decides what they notice, VOICE decides how they say it** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ A second axis: everything before it governs *what* is noticed (domain, attention, position), none of it governs **how they sound**.  A miner talks in effort, a scout in terse present-tense position reports, a hacker obliquely about how a thing is put together, a researcher hedged and unwilling to commit past the evidence.  ⚠⚠ **Personality reaches WORDING and EMPHASIS and never the FACTS** — `@X148` stays absolute, so a blunt person and a hedging one report the same thing and the player extracts the same information from either, just faster from one.  ⚠ The 2023 classes exist and carry a description; **a voice is the new authoring field** | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2h |
| `@X158` | ⚠⚠ **it is a FILTER, never a matrix — or the authoring cost explodes** — writing a line per (class × personality × situation) is combinatorial and kills the feature.  ⚠ Two stages instead: **domain picks a true thing to say, voice colours the phrasing**, so a new class costs its observations and a new voice costs a register — **additive, not multiplicative**.  Same shape as `@X148`'s filter over a true event list with one stage on the end.  ⚠ Two guards: a personality changes how **pleasant or quick** a source is and never **whether it is a source** (or `@X125`'s template choice stops being a decision), and clashing temperaments belong in **chatter and not in mechanics** — a compatibility table touching work efficiency is `@X131`'s affinity bar with extra steps.  ⚠⚠ **And personality is what makes the empty chair hurt**: you do not miss *a scout*, you miss the one who reported in three words | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2h |
| `@X159` | ⚠⚠ **the OFFICE — between missions the player may recruit, dismiss, or give a helper a task at the station** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ `DESIGN.md` § 9 only orders helpers **in-mission**; this is the half that never existed.  ⚠⚠ **The third option is the interesting one**: a helper who stays up runs the store, processes salvage, analyses AI cores or catalogues what came back — and **§ P2a's loop applies unchanged**, because a station task IS work, so it advances the skills it uses.  ⚠ **No "training" verb**: a training menu would be a shortcut around use-based advancement, which `@X124` exists to prevent.  ⚠⚠ **It turns the roster into an ALLOCATION problem every mission** — who goes down is labour and `@X146`'s sensor loadout, who stays up is paid work and **safety**, and the person left in the office is the account you will not have at the debrief | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X160` | ⚠⚠ **dismissal is a real decision because the docs already gave it a dark ending** — firing somebody costs their practice (`@X124`) and their history (`@X131`), but the sharper consequence is `@X113`'s *"personnel can join competitors when not treated well"*: **a dismissed crew member can turn up on a competitor's payroll**, where neglect was the accidental route and dismissal is the deliberate one.  ⚠⚠ **The CHURN hazard** — fire the mediocre, hire fresh, repeat — is `@X125`'s reroll trap with dismissal as the button, and **§ P2a defends it structurally**: twenty sorties of practice beat any fresh template, so churning is bad play rather than a policed move.  ⚠ But that puts weight on *how fast the slow layer rises* — if statistics grow too slowly a veteran is not distinguishably better and the exploit opens after all | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X161` | ⚠ **the office is a PLACE, not a roster screen** — `@X156` holds at the station too: you go to somebody and they say their piece, so **recruiting is going to the person you might hire and dismissing is going to the person you are letting go**, which is what a dismissal should cost.  ⚠ Fast to skip entirely, per `@X120`.  ⚠⚠ **And who is available is who is THERE** — never a refresh button and never a pool that re-rolls on re-entry (`@X125`); it should change for a **reason** (a competitor folded, a rescue brought somebody in), which makes it `@X122`'s *vary the instance* rather than a dice roll with a caption | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X162` | ⚠⚠ **the office is also the MARKET — where salvage becomes money** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ It closes the contract's own loop: `SETTING.md` § The recruitment says **salvage IS the pay** and there was nowhere to be paid; it is the materials→points exchange `MATERIALS.md` § Open questions 1 recommended, and the 2023 knowledge tree already gates it (*"Economy — some items are valuable enough to sell to other star systems"*), so **knowing what is worth selling is DISCOVERED**.  ⚠ **REFINED** — the rule is *not a better way to play the SORTIE game*, aimed at a player who found a better income and stopped landing; **a player who chooses trading as their role outright is `@X165`**.  ⚠⚠ **Prices are STABLE and KNOWABLE**: one that fluctuates on entry is `@X161`'s refresh button on the economy, and waiting for a good day is the reroll trap again.  A price may move for a REASON.  ⚠ Stable prices are also what make in-mission salvage choices legible | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X163` | ⚠⚠ **the market is where MULTIPLAYER finally has a PLACE** — `DESIGN.md` § 20 has carried multiplayer as an ambition with **no interface anywhere in the design**.  ⚠⚠ **A shared market needs none of the netcode a shared sortie would**: players never have to be in the same base at the same time, so it is **asynchronous** and fits § 14's bounded one-sitting sessions exactly — the office is what sits in the gaps.  ⚠ So the first multiplayer feature is not co-op but **two players who never meet, trading through a station**; the second is § 20's abandoned-base inheritance, the same asynchrony one layer out | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X164` | ⚠⚠ **KNOWLEDGE IS NOT TRADEABLE, and it is a hard line** — materials, salvage, equipment and tower-tops may cross between players; knowledge may not.  `@X117` makes what the player knows the DOMINANT progression and `@X118` forbids buying answers, so a player who could purchase *"a gate buys nothing"* would be buying the axis the design rests on.  ⚠ **The same applies to NPC vendors**, and it is the office's version of `@X155`: **the office carries the OFFICIAL world and the planet carries the real one** — a trader may quote the price of lithium, nobody at the station can tell you what is under the buried city.  ⚠ **Crew are an open maybe**: `@X160` already sends a dismissed helper to a competitor, so a helper changing employer between players is that event with a name on it.  ⚠⚠ **CLOSED 2026-08-28** (`@X290`): crew may NOT be traded — `@X214`'s *you sold a COPY, not the thing* cannot apply to a person, a crew market is `@X125`'s reroll trap with money, and it would price the courtesy `@X169` needs to be free.  The version that survives is `@X168`'s: **the person chooses, never the employers** | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X165` | ⚠⚠ **a pure TRADER player is legitimate, and the role has to be POLITICAL** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"not against a pure trader player, but that has to become a political role in station too.  And it should not be a spreadsheet role"*).  ⚠ The exploit and the role differ in what the game is MADE of — margins versus politics.  ⚠⚠ **`@X162`'s stable prices are what FORCE it to be political**: with no arbitrage, no market timing and nothing to buy low and sell high, **the only lever left is WHO you deal with** — so a rule written against the reroll trap is what makes *not a spreadsheet role* achievable rather than aspirational.  ⚠ The material is already in `SETTING.md`: permits (*"permit on file = pass; permit missing = destroyed"*), competitors approved and not, the cordon's own officials, nine factions with `economy` among them, and the armada that comes *"when somehow forced to"* | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X166` | ⚠⚠ **the trader CANNOT hollow out the game, because it feeds on it** — salvage is produced by sorties and by nothing else, so **a trader with no sortie-runners has nothing to trade**.  That is structural rather than a rule.  ⚠ A trader's scarce resource is not money but **whose side they are on**, spent once per decision in front of people who remember — recognisably `@X131`'s ledger one layer out.  ⚠ They may broker **contracts, access, supply and permits**; they may **not** broker knowledge (`@X164`), which is the natural trader fantasy and exactly the forbidden one: brokering *who lands where* is fine, brokering *what they will find* is selling the dominant axis | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X167` | ⚠⚠ **RECORDED HONESTLY: the trader is the one part of this design that is NOT a tower defence** — `DESIGN.md` § And the DEEP layers asks whether a thing resolves into position, terrain or timing, and a trader's game resolves into **relationships and consequences**.  It does not pass and is not meant to: it is a different ROLE, not a mechanic inside the tower defence.  ⚠⚠ **The boundary that matters: the trader role must not change the tower-defence game for the people playing it** — they may decide who gets supplied, never gain a lever that reaches into somebody else's base, alters their waves or plays their sortie.  ⚠ Written down rather than smoothed over, because the next contributor will otherwise delete it for failing the genre test or let it grow into the main game.  ⚠ It is naturally a **multiplayer** role: single player would need NPC operators generating real supply and demand, which is the economic simulation `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` already refuses | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2i |
| `@X168` | ⚠⚠ **THE CREW SECTION — another player may pick up who you left, and they may not come back** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ Half of it is already in `DESIGN.md` § 9 § Stranded helpers (*"or (multiplayer) for a different player who lands nearby"*); what is new is **what happens after the pickup** — the ledger cashed in, so a relation that is *frankly bad* lets them stay with their rescuer.  ⚠⚠ **It answers `@X164`'s open question and better than trading would: crew move between players, but never as GOODS** — not a transaction but a **choice**, through abandonment and rescue rather than the market.  **You cannot buy somebody's crew; you can only be the one who came for them.**  ⚠ And it needs **no new mechanic** — `plans/14`'s retrieval path is shipped, so a cross-player rescue is that path with a different name on the record | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2e |
| `@X169` | ⚠⚠ **the default must be RETURN, or multiplayer becomes a punishment** — if defection were common, leaving anybody behind would read as being robbed for playing multiplayer.  The owner's calibration is the word *frankly*: the ordinary case is **a courtesy**, and defection is rare and EARNED out of a relation that was already bad.  ⚠⚠ **Which makes returning a helper a favour with NO mechanical reward** — so a player who reliably brings people home acquires a **reputation**, and reputation is `@X165`'s political currency arriving on a second channel for free.  ⚠ The choice reads out of the LEDGER and stays legible (`@X131` forbids a bar): *"you left me twice, he came for me"*, never *loyalty 34%*, and never a dice roll at the moment of rescue | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2e |
| `@X170` | ⚠ **what crosses is a PERSON, not an ANSWER — so `@X164` survives intact.**  A defector carries practice (`@X124`) and history (`@X131`) and what they learned alongside their old employer (`@X151`), which looks like forbidden knowledge transfer and is not: they **report without prescribing** (`@X149`) and **point without concluding** (`@X129`), so the new employer gains **a colleague with a history elsewhere** rather than a rule — *"that's like a base I was on before"*, about a base the new player never saw.  ⚠⚠ **It degrades to single player with no second design**: without other players the rescuer is a competitor, which is `@X160` already — one mechanic, two audiences.  ⚠ And it is a **second asynchronous channel** (`@X163`): the two players never have to meet | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2e |
| `@X171` | ⚠⚠ **being left behind is NOT necessarily grim — a helper may build a life** (owner, 2026-08-26): shelter and a vegetable garden out of what the wildlife killed, filters for the air, waiting the robots out.  ⚠ The fiction was ready — `SETTING.md` § Nobody is attacking anybody means **a lone human with no jammer is not a target**, § History gives the natives' air filters, `@X100`'s pollen litters the map with machines nobody is coming for, and § The settlers today have run this playbook for generations.  ⚠⚠ **How well they do is a READOUT OF THE CREW YOU BUILT** — `scrounge`, `build`, `mine`, `stamina`, every one raised by work the player gave them (`@X124`), so the homestead is the practice loop's own verdict delivered back as a place.  ⚠⚠ **It changes the TONE and not the COST**: the player still loses their labour, their debrief account and their practice — what changes is the guilt, which turns going back from a duty into a want.  ⚠ And it opens a THIRD ending beside return and defection: **they stay**, which the 2023 notes already anticipate (*"most settlers don't want to leave"*) | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2e |
| `@X172` | ⚠⚠ **hunting for other players' stranded helpers is CONTENT, not economy** — a third asynchronous channel after the market and the rescue, and the first that is not trade.  ⚠⚠ **Its point is the last clause of the owner's idea: *let them see what is possible*** — a homestead built out of parts in a place the finder thought unsurvivable **TEACHES**, and it teaches the only way `@X137`'s no-tutorial ruling permits.  ⚠ Authored by play rather than by hand, which makes it the cheapest content in the design and the only kind that could not have been written in advance | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2e |
| `@X173` | ⚠⚠ **player-built CACHES — abandonment as a PLAN** (owner, 2026-08-26).  A rocket has limited weight and volume, so there is always more on the ground than will lift: bury the overflow as a **kickstart for a later mission near the same place**.  ⚠ It **sharpens** the scramble rather than easing it — the manifest becomes *what do I take, what do I hide, can I get back to it*.  ⚠⚠ **Other players may find one and crack it open, "not easy"** — PvP with no combat and no netcode.  ⚠ Three rules against griefing: hard to find, hard to crack, and ⚠⚠ **cracking should be LOUD**, a lockpick that draws the planet's attention, which makes robbing somebody a decision rather than a chore.  ⚠ Ownership stays legible, because it feeds `@X169`'s reputation | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X174` | ⚠⚠ **THE PLANET ACCUMULATES OTHER PEOPLE'S PLAY** — an abandoned base, a cache (`@X173`) and a stranded helper's homestead (`@X171`) are **one shape**: things a player leaves behind that persist, that another player finds, and that nobody authored.  ⚠ Each is a consequence made physical, and together they are the **cheapest content in the design** — generated by people playing, impossible to write in advance, and ⚠⚠ **it teaches**, which is the only kind of lesson § There is NO TUTORIAL permits.  ⚠ All three want per-planet persistence and none wants netcode beyond a shared store | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X175` | ⚠⚠ **TOWERS ARE ALWAYS STRONGER THAN A PLAYER — so a base is IMPREGNABLE TO A PERSON** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ Not a new rule: `DESIGN.md` § 8 already makes the player a noncombatant with no weapon and § 7 makes towers the things that fight — this is that, applied to two players.  ⚠⚠ Therefore **PvP cannot be raiding-by-violence**: the only way into somebody's base is **invitation** (friendly towers), and the only way against them is **the planet** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X176` | ⚠⚠ **the aggressive route runs through the ROBOT ECONOMY, not through combat** — *"unless you can hack enough robots to overwhelm another player"*.  You do not attack a player, **you point the planet at them**, which preserves the genre rather than bending it: the enemy is still robots, the defence is still towers and walls.  ⚠ Every piece exists — § 9's hacking helpers, `MATERIALS.md`'s AI core (takeable, never manufacturable), the `hack` skill's levels, `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md`'s routes.  ⚠⚠ **Deliberately EXPENSIVE**: *enough* robots is a campaign-scale investment, so hostility is decided over sorties and never launched on a whim — `SETTING.md` § Nobody is attacking anybody holding at the multiplayer layer.  ⚠⚠ **And the attack arrives as CONTENT, not griefing**: the defender's answer is towers, walls, position and timing, so nothing arrives they have no vocabulary for | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X177` | ⚠⚠ **the DEFAULT PvP is a RACE, not a fight** — *"other than gathering resources quicker than they are"* — two operators working the same region, contesting **the environment around the bases**, which is where this game already spends its time.  ⚠⚠ **And it SETTLES `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § Open questions 1: per-PLANET**, because you cannot compete for a mine that only exists inside your own instance.  That question's own recommendation (author per-map, keep node identifiers global) is the migration path | `DESIGN.md` § 20, `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` |
| `@X178` | ⚠⚠ **co-op is FREE, because every action is position-triggered** — *"unless they set their towers to friendly for you"* is a permission, and once given the feature is already built: § 11 § Position triggers makes every action a place rather than a keypress, and **none of them asks who you are**.  A guest repairs towers (20 s of standing there), clears bodies (`salvage_at` takes a hex) and delivers salvage (driving over it).  ⚠⚠ **So letting somebody in IS the co-op feature** — no shared-control model, no party system, no second input path.  ⚠ Permission is a **TOWER STATE**, which § HUD already speaks as a colour, so it is diegetic and legible across the base — and revocable, which makes **betrayal possible** and the trust worth something.  ⚠ Stays asynchronous-capable: an attack on an offline player is the abandoned-base bullet one step over | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X179` | ⚠⚠ **TOWER HACKING is scoped to 2v2 / 3v3 MATCHES, where whole bases can be taken over** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ It is **the one thing that would break `@X175`** — turning a tower makes the base's own strength the attacker, and being killed by your own perimeter is not a wave anybody can answer with towers and walls.  ⚠⚠ **The team scoping is what makes it safe, and the reason is CONSENT plus a COUNTER**: in a match both teams signed up and a **teammate can drive to a hacked tower**, where in the open world a campaign base is at stake and possibly nobody can answer.  ⚠ *Open-world PvP is something that happens to you; a match is something you entered.*  ⚠⚠ **And a takeover is a contest of PRESENCE** — § 7's repair is 20 s of standing there, so **hacking and repairing are the same verb pointed in opposite directions**, and § 8's noncombatant player survives: you take a base by BEING at its towers, never by shooting | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X180` | ⚠ **the first SYNCHRONOUS feature in the design** — the market, the crew rescue, caches and even an attack on an offline player are all asynchronous, but **a base takeover needs both sides present**, so it wants real netcode rather than a shared store, plus a match lifecycle, teams and a win condition.  ⚠ It still fits § 14's **bounded one-sitting** shape (a match is exactly that) but not its solo structure, and small teams are what keep both the netcode and the base scale legible | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X181` | ⚠⚠ **it must not leak into the campaign world** — a match played over a campaign base would delete `@X175`'s impregnability by the back door.  *Recommendation: **matches are their own container*** — bring a crew and a loadout, risk neither the base you have been building nor the people: **match casualties do not carry**, or a lost match costs a crew member with a campaign's practice and history on them (`@X131`), which is a stake nobody consented to when they queued.  ⚠ Tower hacking stays a **MATCH verb** until somebody argues it into the persistent world, and that case must answer **what a solo player does when their perimeter turns on them with no teammate to send** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X182` | ⚠⚠ **NETCODE IS GATED ON DEMAND, and the competitive mode is SEPARATE from the campaign** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ It draws a clean line — **asynchronous vs synchronous**: the market, crew rescue, caches, homesteads and attacks on offline bases all ship on **persistence alone** (a shared store), while only team matches and base takeover need netcode.  ⚠⚠ **So netcode is not on the critical path at all**, and the roadmap carries five multiplayer features with no networking phase in front of them.  ⚠ **And the deferral PROTECTS the campaign design**: building netcode early would bias every decision toward synchronicity — bases designed for matches, waves balanced for teams — where deferring keeps the campaign primary and makes the match mode INHERIT from it | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X183` | ⚠ **the trigger is a shipped game with players asking for COMPETITIVE play specifically** — not co-op, and not *"multiplayer"* in general, because the asynchronous half already is multiplayer.  ⚠⚠ **And the trigger may never fire**: trading through a station, racing for a mine, cracking a cache and taking somebody's abandoned crew are **already non-cooperative**, so the cheap features may absorb the demand — which would be a good outcome rather than a disappointing one.  ⚠⚠ **Do not be fooled by loft shipping netcode**: that makes the TRANSPORT cheap, while the expensive part is the DESIGN — match lifecycle, teams, matchmaking, win conditions, balance and anti-grief, none of which the campaign needs and all of which would be maintained for ever | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X184` | ⚠⚠ **a TERRITORIAL mode — hackable installations, contested, both sides building defences** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ **It is a REARRANGEMENT rather than a new game**: `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md`'s six installation types are the neutral structures, `@X176`'s hacking is the way to take them, and towers and walls are the defences — the core game unchanged.  ⚠⚠ **It collides with `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § No underground level, and the container is the resolution**: that refusal was made for the CAMPAIGN and still stands there, while a separate mode may afford a second movement model precisely because it does not serve the campaign — the first real payoff of `@X181`/`@X182`'s separation.  ⚠ Cheaper version worth trying first: **entrances on the surface map**, territorial without descending | `DESIGN.md` § 20, `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` |
| `@X185` | ⚠⚠ **THE BALANCE RULE: a big area is much harder to guard, so the leader is automatically more vulnerable to PRECISE attacks** — a catch-up mechanic **with no rubber band**, because nobody is given anything: the leader's advantage IS their weakness, by geometry.  ⚠⚠ **And dryopea has already measured it** — `@M020`'s siege front is the wall's WIDTH, `plans/12` § B7's sealed-vs-gated clock, `plans/17` § T3's shuttling-vs-parked upkeep, and `PROGRESSION.md` § P7's compact-vs-spread tension.  ⚠⚠ **The geometry enforces it because installations CANNOT BE MOVED** — a mine is where the ore is, so holdings are inherently spread and there is no consolidating move; the leader must **choose** what to defend.  ⚠ *"Precise"* is load-bearing: the trailing player needs **better targeting, not more force**, which puts `@X117`'s dominant axis in charge of a competitive match | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X186` | ⚠ **two things to tune rather than assume** in the territorial mode — **expansion must be worth the exposure** (if defending at scale is impossible, nobody expands and there is no equilibrium), and **precision must cost too** (or the trailing player harasses for ever; `@X176`'s *hacking is slow and expensive* is what makes a precise attack a spent investment rather than a free poke) | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X187` | ⚠⚠ **the multiplayer block is a DESTINATION, not a QUEUE, and its dependency is an AUDIENCE rather than a technology** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"it needs an active community of people that only the base game can provide"*).  ⚠⚠ **Every mode is cheap BECAUSE it reuses the base game's assets and mechanics — and that reuse only works if the base game is finished and coherent first.**  ⚠ So the length of `DESIGN.md` § 20 is **not a signal of its priority**: it accumulated in one sitting because the ideas compose, and `plans/ROADMAP.md` § The critical path is unchanged with **BUILDING still the biggest missing mechanic**.  ⚠⚠ **What serves these modes today is building the base game well** — a mechanic bent early to suit a mode with no players yet is the way to lose both | `DESIGN.md` § 20, `plans/ROADMAP.md` |
| `@X188` | ⚠⚠ **building out the CAMPAIGN brings multiplayer much closer — not by attracting players, but because it builds the PIECES** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ Each for its own reason: **per-planet persistence** (Tier D) is the **sole prerequisite of five features**; the **robot economy** (Tier E) is the territorial mode's neutral structures; **hacking** (Tier C) is `@X176`'s whole aggressive route; the **crew layer** is cross-player rescue and defection entire; the **station office** is the market and the trader.  ⚠⚠ **So the distance shrinks as a SIDE EFFECT** — the co-op permission (a tower colour) is close to the only new part, because § 11's position triggers already make every action ownership-agnostic.  ⚠⚠ **The one actionable rule: do not gold-plate a campaign piece for multiplayer, and do not design one that FORECLOSES it** — keep persistence per-planet-per-player rather than per-session, and keep code asking *which* player rather than assuming *the* player (`salvage_at` takes a hex and `blocker_at` answers which vehicle, so today's is healthy) | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X189` | ⚠ **the advancement RATE: bounded per session, and slower where a skill is already deep** (owner, 2026-08-26) — *"they still progress but probably only after multiple sessions"*.  ⚠ **Two rules, two problems**: the per-session bound stops one long base transforming somebody and stops grinding inside a run; the depth curve stops a veteran running away.  ⚠ The second is half-designed already — `@X124`'s fast skills / slow statistics is the same instinct applied to the LAYER, and this adds the curve WITHIN each.  ⚠ The first composes with `@X126`: a session's gain is bounded **and** conditional on boarding.  ⚠ A shape worth considering — let `@X113`'s **endurance pools** do the per-session bounding, so the limit EMERGES (a tired person works less, so learns less) rather than being a hard cap with a cliff that makes short sessions efficient | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X190` | ⚠⚠ **RESOLVED 2026-08-26 by `@X191`** — ~~open: diminishing returns and the anti-churn defence pull opposite ways~~.  The collision assumed the curve was on **growth**; the owner's answer puts it on **effect**, so nothing converges and `@X160`'s defence survives.  ⚠ Kept because the reasoning is the reusable part: *a depth curve on growth would let a replacement reach good enough in a few sessions, which is `@X125`'s reroll trap arriving through the maths rather than through a button* | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X191` | ⚠⚠ **there is NO numerical ceiling — the diminishing return is on the EFFECT, not on the growth** (owner, 2026-08-26): *"longer detection ranges do not matter too much when they are already high"*.  ⚠ So the number keeps moving, a veteran and a fresh hire never converge, and `@X160`'s anti-churn defence survives intact — while a hire's early gains land on the **steepest part of the utility curve** and feel useful at once.  ⚠⚠ **And the excess is not wasted, it SPILLS**: `@X124`'s loop means practice never lands on one skill, so a veteran who saturated their specialism spent the surplus **becoming broadly capable**.  ⚠⚠ **The difference between veteran and newcomer stops being LEVEL and becomes BREADTH** — a better answer than a numerical gap, and already in the design rather than invented for it.  ⚠ It also makes saturation **the reason the lattice exists**: without it a player specialises for ever, with it over-investment converts into range | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X192` | ⚠⚠ **SATURATION BEGINS WHERE THE SKILL STOPS BEING THE BOTTLENECK** — a test rather than a taste, and it is how each skill's curve gets authored: find what the skill competes against and taper it where the other thing takes over.  ⚠ Both owner examples are that shape (*the job is already fully served*): detection range saturates when you already see what you need **before you need it**; build speed saturates where **helper-seconds stop being the bottleneck**, which `DESIGN.md` § 13 names as the wall's constraint.  ⚠⚠ **And it gives the allocation problem a second dimension** — keeping a saturated specialist on the same job is waste, so *who learns from it* joins *who can do it*, and giving somebody a job they are worse at costs you **now** and pays **later** | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X193` | ⚠⚠ **SPECIALISATION MUST BEAT BREADTH — a GLOBAL slowdown on TOTAL learning** (owner, 2026-08-26): *"everything will hinder the helper too, so we can add a general reduction in learning speed when they have learned a lot, **even for skills that are low**"*.  ⚠ A second curve on a different axis — `@X191` is per SKILL (more of a high skill buys little), this is **per PERSON**.  ⚠⚠ **The arithmetic: the same total learning buys more when concentrated** — spread it and the total rises fast, everything slows and nothing is deep enough to be worth its plateau.  ⚠⚠ **It AMENDS `@X191`'s breadth conclusion**: `@X124`'s spill still gives a specialist modest breadth, but that is the **REWARD for depth, not an alternative to it** — *breadth comes from depth*.  ⚠ The fiction is `@X157`'s already: an experienced person has **habits**, so expertise is a lens that focuses and narrows at once | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X194` | ⚠⚠ **and it is what stops the roster converging on six interchangeable veterans** — without it the endgame optimum is six people good at everything and `@X159`'s allocation problem evaporates; with it **the roster is heterogeneous by construction** and the six-person cap means something.  ⚠ **Losing a specialist hurts more** (you cannot retrain into the gap), which sharpens the empty chair, the stranded helper and defection at once.  ⚠⚠ **And it separates RECRUITING from CHURN by itself**: a fresh hire has a low total and therefore **learns fast**, so hiring to fill a gap no veteran can grow into is good play, while hiring to *replace* still loses deep specialists for novices (`@X160`).  ⚠ Two to settle: whether **spill counts toward the total** (*recommendation: count everything, keep spill small — one accumulator beats two, and a generalist accumulates faster either way*), and that **the slowdown must never reach zero**, or a veteran reads as a dead character | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X195` | ⚠⚠ **NO GREY SOUP, but there must be a REASON to broaden — and the LATTICE already is one** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ Both failures are real and opposite: everybody-with-everything, and everybody-with-one-skill (a helper idle whenever their one job is not needed **where they are**).  ⚠⚠ **It was never breadth-versus-depth — it is WHICH breadth**: `@X124`'s lattice prices **adjacent** breadth (a skill sharing a statistic with the specialism) as **pre-paid by spill**, and **distant** breadth (sharing nothing) at full price against `@X193`'s slowdown.  ⚠⚠ **Every skill has exactly 6 cheap neighbours and 5 expensive strangers**, read off the lattice rather than invented — so a specialist becomes half a generalist along their own two statistics for free, and the other half is what costs | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X196` | ⚠⚠ **the design target has a SHAPE: a mature helper is deep in 2-3 STATISTICS and the skills hanging off them — a CLUSTER**, not one skill and not twelve.  ⚠ Six statistics, so a person is characterised by which few they live in — **plan/tinker** is the engineer, **observe/stamina** the field hand, **charisma/agility** the nimble talker.  ⚠⚠ Neither soup nor a one-trick, and it needs **no new mechanism**: the lattice produces it once `@X193` prices distant breadth.  ⚠ The reasons to broaden are already load-bearing — the strongest is **a person is in ONE PLACE at a time**, which `plans/17` § T3 measured (shuttling helpers clear 205 robots, the same two parked lose the base), plus the six-person cap, redundancy against loss, and `@X146`'s second kind of account | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2a2 |
| `@X197` | ⚠⚠ **ASSIGNMENT IS A PILLAR, and SCARCITY is what keeps it one** (owner, 2026-08-26): *"it should never be totally clear what they should do, and there are always more tasks that need attention than there are helpers around"*.  ⚠ It is `DESIGN.md` § What kind of game this is at the **management layer** — the scarce thing is **a person**, and the cost of an assignment is everything they are not doing.  ⚠⚠ **The scarcity is STRUCTURAL and needs no throttle**: a tower's charge decays per shot, a bigger perimeter is more wall to brace, kills pile into ramps somebody must clear, salvage decays.  **Growth creates its own demand** — `@X185`'s *a big area is harder to guard*, inside one base.  ⚠⚠ So **the 6-helper cap is not a limit to be overcome, it is the mechanism**: a base big enough to want seven generates work for nine | `DESIGN.md` § 9 |
| `@X198` | ⚠⚠ ***never totally clear* needs FOUR axes that do not reduce to each other** — **value-kind** (repair pays in defence, clearing in access, building in future defence, salvage in points: different currencies), **information** (`@X122` varies the instance, `@X129`'s crew point without concluding), **growth** (`@X192` — the job somebody is worse at costs now and pays later, so the obvious person is not obviously right) and **position**.  ⚠⚠ **The test: does a proposed change make ONE axis dominate?**  If so the decision becomes clear and the pillar dies.  ⚠ Named failure modes: **a priority-ranked task list** (the game doing the player's thinking, `@X118`), ⚠⚠ **auto-assignment** (*"helpers pick their own work"* — deletes the pillar, and the quality-of-life feature most likely to be requested), one task that always pays best, and a person strictly best at everything (already prevented by `@X193`) | `DESIGN.md` § 9 |
| `@X199` | ⚠ **it SHARPENS `@X142`'s idle helper rather than contradicting it** — the apparent collision is *if there is always more work than people, how is anybody idle?*  ⚠⚠ **Because idle is a MANAGEMENT state, not a world state**: a helper stands waiting because **nobody gave them a job**, and with obvious work in every direction *"nobody has given me a task"* is a **sharper** complaint, not a weaker one.  ⚠ It also explains the onboarding case exactly — on landing nobody has orders yet, so the whole crew is idle **while the work is already visible** | `DESIGN.md` § 9 |
| `@X200` | ⚠⚠ **THE SPREADSHEET TEST — the economy must not be solvable, EVEN WITH THE SOURCE** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ That clause sets the bar far above *do not show the numbers*: this repo is public, so **any defence made of secrecy has already failed** and the economy must resist optimisation **by its shape**.  ⚠⚠ **The test: can a player write down a single number to maximise?**  If yes, they will, and the design has failed.  ⚠ It is `@X198` moved one system over — the rule for helper assignment applied to the economy | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The spreadsheet test |
| `@X201` | ⚠⚠ **AMENDED 2026-08-26 by `@X203`** — ~~hiding the numbers fails~~ applies to the **ECONOMY only**.  ⚠ For **helper skills** hiding is the RIGHT answer, because it buys **character** rather than unsolvability: knowing *"repair 34"* still leaves `@X198`'s four incommensurable axes, so the number does not solve the decision, and a character screen would replace people with rows (`@X157`).  ⚠⚠ **What fails EVERYWHERE is randomness**: a spreadsheet just computes the expected value, so it makes the optimum *easier* to state — it replaces a hard question with an average.  It is the instinctive answer and it is the wrong one | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The spreadsheet test |
| `@X202` | ⚠⚠ **what actually defends it: NO SCALAR OBJECTIVE**, plus three supports.  A spreadsheet needs something to maximise, and this design already pays in currencies with **no exchange rate** — `DESIGN.md` § Scouting's *every reward has its own pressure*: crystal wakes elementals, cutting a route reroutes it **through you**, starving a factory wakes the military, sap invites an insect chase.  ⚠ Supports: **the world REACTS** (a computed optimum invalidates itself), **the instance is not the rules** (`@X122` — a spreadsheet says what to look for, never what is there), and **the decisions are WHERE and WHEN rather than HOW MUCH**.  ⚠⚠ **Honest check: *a static graph plus a rate per edge* is spreadsheet-shaped** — what saves it is that the graph is not what the player optimises.  ⚠⚠ **So the thing to watch is: never add a resource that is purely good.**  One reward with no pressure attached is a scalar, and a scalar is a spreadsheet's foothold | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The spreadsheet test |
| `@X203` | ⚠⚠ **the HELPERS and the ECONOMY need DIFFERENT answers** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"to the helpers it is more about hiding numbers"*) — hiding a helper's stats buys **feel** and needs no defence against a source-reader, because the number never solved the assignment; hiding the economy's would be an attempt at **unsolvability**, and that is the one that fails | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The spreadsheet test, `PROGRESSION.md` § P2h |
| `@X204` | ⚠⚠ **the MECHANISM is individual NPC TRADERS on their own daily business** (owner, 2026-08-26) — *"they do not really adhere to the general trend but to their daily activities, so there is a general trend but it is hidden enough"*.  ⚠⚠ **It is not the randomness `@X201` refused**: randomness is a distribution you SAMPLE and a spreadsheet averages it, while a trader is **a person you VISIT** — there is nothing to average because you are going to *one* of them, and what they are doing today has a **reason**.  ⚠⚠ So the variation becomes **positional and temporal rather than statistical**, which is `@X202`'s fourth support made concrete.  ⚠⚠ **It reconciles with `@X162`**: the **trend** is the stable, learnable, player-influenced part you plan a sortie on; an **individual** deviates, and that is not a re-roll because you cannot refresh a person — going to another is **a trip**.  ***You can plan on the trend; you cannot compute the result.***  ⚠ Traders are the economy's **partial sensors**, the `@X129` pattern applied to a system — and it is what gives `@X165`'s trader role a face.  ⚠ **Honest limit**: the goal is *the spreadsheet is not worth writing*, never *impossible* | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The spreadsheet test |
| `@X205` | ⚠⚠ **TRUST per trader affects pricing, which is what stops *shop around* being the answer** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ Without it the trader design has an obvious solution — **visit them all and take the best price** — a shopping algorithm that turns `@X204`'s positional variation back into a solvable search.  ⚠⚠ Trust closes it: **the question stops being *where is the best price* and becomes *who am I doing business with*** — a search becomes a **commitment**.  ⚠ Same LEDGER as `@X131`, one system over: no affinity bar, no number on screen, just what happened (*"I have sold to her for six sorties"*).  ⚠ And it makes the market political from **both** sides — `@X165` gives the trader-player *who they deal with*, this gives the sortie-player the same lever back.  ⚠ The hard part is a **now-versus-later** trade: best price today versus best price over the campaign, two currencies with no exchange rate | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The spreadsheet test |
| `@X206` | ⚠⚠ **and TRADERS MUST SPECIALISE, or loyalty becomes automatically optimal** — if trust only accumulates, the play is *pick one early and never switch*, a dominant strategy that kills the decision after the first sortie.  ⚠⚠ One deals metals, another chemicals, another is simply somewhere else, so **your trusted metals buyer is no help when you come back with sap** and the switching question is live every time the cargo changes.  Same shape as `@X196`'s helper clusters, one system over.  ⚠ Second hazard — trust *everywhere* — wants trust to accrue **slowly enough that a campaign deepens two or three**; the harder version (dealing with one COSTS standing with another) is more political and more frustrating, so keep it in reserve.  ⚠ Related to `@X169`'s reputation but **not the same thing** — that is general standing, this is per-person; they should feed each other, and collapsing them into one *social score* is the affinity bar `@X131` refuses | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The spreadsheet test |
| `@X207` | ⚠⚠ **FACTIONS, and the relations BETWEEN them, inside and outside the station — *a living ecosystem instead*** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ **The word doing the work is *instead*: a SYSTEM is something a player solves, an ECOSYSTEM is something they live in.**  ⚠ What it adds is a **WEB where today there is a HUB** — everything so far is player↔X, and factions add X↔Y, so **the player becomes one node among many rather than the centre**.  ⚠ Nothing needs inventing: the nine factions and their tensions are already in `SETTING.md` and the 2023 data; what is missing is only that **they have no relations with each other**.  ⚠⚠ **And it finishes the spreadsheet argument** — dealing with a trader now also moves you with their rival and their rival's ally, so a spreadsheet would need the whole web AND its dynamics.  ⚠⚠ **No faction standings screen** — that is `@X131`'s affinity bar at ecosystem scale; **you learn where you stand by how people TREAT you** | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X208` | ⚠ **two constraints on the faction web** — ⚠⚠ **reactive, never STRATEGIC**: `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § What this design does NOT do refuses AI strategy (*"the escalation ladder is a threshold, and rerouting is a shortest-path recompute"*), so relations respond **to what the player did**, by threshold and tally, and **never plan against them**.  ⚠ And **sparse and AUTHORED, never a matrix** — nine factions is 36 pairs and most never touch, so author **a handful of live tensions** the way § Open questions 3 prefers named alternates over computed detours, because the player needs the web **learnable** | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X209` | ⚠⚠ **THE THREAD'S ONE SENTENCE: each rule removes a way to COMPUTE and puts a way to LIVE in its place.**  `@X198` four incommensurable axes → no objective function; `@X202` every reward has its pressure → no free gain; `@X204` traders you visit → positional, not statistical; `@X205` trust makes switching a commitment → relationship, not search; `@X207` factions relate to each other → the player is not the centre.  ⚠ **Keep this when judging anything added to the economy later** — and note the **partial-sensor pattern now appears three times** (the crew for the world, traders for the market, factions for the ecosystem), which makes it a house pattern rather than a coincidence | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X210` | ⚠⚠ **a good deal for the TRADER is an investment — extracting maximum value is short-sighted** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ The asymmetry is what makes it a decision: **the cost is VISIBLE** (you see the worse price you took) and **the payoff is NOT** (standing is never a number on screen), arriving later as *being served when supply is tight*.  ⚠ It is `DESIGN.md` § What kind of game this is at the social layer — the thing spent is **a margin you chose not to take**.  ⚠⚠ And it becomes clear the way everything else does: through **consequence** (the trader who has stock for you, or does not sell it to your rival) and through **partial reports** — the first time a rival gets the supply you needed, you understand | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X211` | ⚠⚠ **the station is COMPETITIVE and NON-VIOLENT — *"no PvP, but a shared resource system"*** — which is `@X175` one layer up: the competition is entirely social and economic.  ⚠ What is genuinely scarce: a trader's **stock** (finite), their **priority** when supply is tight, **permits** (the chokepoint of the whole fiction), standing with a **particular** trader (positional — *the* trusted operator is a place only one can hold), and regions of the planet (`@X177`).  ⚠⚠ **The guard against starving anybody out: the planet is the PRIMARY TAP.**  Salvage comes from sorties and nothing else (`@X166`), so a player denied at the station can always go and get more — **the station is a multiplier, never the only source**, and it must never become a substitute for going down.  ⚠ Degrades to single player unchanged: the rivals are `SETTING.md` § The competitors | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X212` | ⚠⚠ ***WINNING IS BEING A BIGGER PART OF THE ECOSYSTEM — like winning in a democracy, never a fixed condition*** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ Better than *a series of contests*: § The capstone makes the player **one node among many**, so **winning is CENTRALITY IN THE WEB, not victory over anybody**.  ⚠ The analogy is a rule list — you can win an election but never win *democracy*; influence is a **degree**; ⚠⚠ **it must be MAINTAINED, so standing DECAYS**; and your position moves when **others** act, so you can become less central having done nothing wrong.  ⚠⚠ **Decay is not a new mechanic — it is this game's signature applied socially** (a tower's charge, salvage, bodies), and it is a **better** answer to `@X206`'s *trust everywhere* hazard than slow accrual: maintenance costs trips and deals, so you cannot hold every relationship at once.  ⚠ Preserves `DESIGN.md` § 14 exactly: *more central* and *less central* are the only readings and **neither is an ending**.  ⚠ **No rank, no leaderboard, no score** — you know your influence by what happens when you ask for something | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X213` | ⚠⚠ **a LIGHT POLITICAL SYSTEM — centrality converts into INVESTMENT** (owner, 2026-08-26): players influence **where bigger investments are done**, perhaps even **a second station**.  ⚠⚠ **This is what makes `@X212`'s centrality matter** — without it being central is a feeling, with it it is a lever over shared money.  ⚠ Genuinely political because the investment is **collective**: nobody can do it alone, it affects everybody, and players with holdings in different regions want it in different places — **a real conflict of interest with no bad actors**.  ⚠⚠ ***"Light"* means NO SEPARATE INTERFACE: you do not cast a ballot, your accumulated position IS the input** — your trade, your standing, the deals you took worse terms on.  Participating in it is what you were already doing, which also gives `@X210` its visible long-term payoff | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X214` | ⚠⚠ **INFORMATION has value for NPCs, never between players** (owner, 2026-08-26) — which **resolves the tension `@X164` created**: player→player hands somebody an answer they should have earned, while **player→NPC converts your own discovery into value and you KEEP the knowledge — you sold a copy, not the thing**.  ⚠⚠ It makes `@X102`'s knowledge tree **economically live for the first time** without making it purchasable: you still cannot *buy* a fact, only **sell** one you found.  ⚠ The named pieces are all already in `SETTING.md` and each is a truth the cordon lacks — **humans on the planet** (who hide from players too), **the robots are steered not haywire** (the cordon is built around a phantom), **the buried city**, and **the art inside it** (which has two values: the artefacts, and the knowledge that they exist).  ⚠ § Future contact's *"serious leverage"*, given a price | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X215` | ⚠⚠ **THE DECAY IS THE STRONGEST ANTI-OPTIMISATION RULE HERE — information *loses value when the common public knows*, so a piece pays ONCE, EVER, across the whole player base.**  ⚠ First to sell takes it, so the race is in **discovery** rather than grinding; selling destroys the future value for everybody, so the choice is *take it now* against *it is worth more while I am the only one who knows*; and ⚠⚠ **it cannot be farmed — a resource existing in quantity ONE has nothing to optimise**, which satisfies `@X200` outright rather than defending against it.  ⚠⚠ It carries its own pressure as `@X202` requires: revealing *there are humans down there* is **a betrayal with a price tag** and **cannot be undone**.  ⚠ The mystery survives because late sellers get nothing and `@X155`/`@X150` keep supply slow — ⚠ but **price it as significant and not transformative**, or somebody sells the setting for cash on day one | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X216` | ⚠⚠ **WORLD EVENTS are a TRIGGER SYSTEM, not a schedule** (owner, 2026-08-26; technical model: `../crew_punk/BLOCKS.md` § The campaign is a trigger system).  ***Nothing is scheduled, everything is ELIGIBLE*** — content fires when its conditions become true, and *"nobody decides what happens next, the system evaluates what has become possible"*.  ⚠⚠ **dryopea has spent this whole design building the trigger INPUTS**: world events are not new content but **the firing mechanism for consequences the docs already promised** — the cordon reacting to sold information (`@X214`), the armada on denied throughput, elementals stirring on deep extraction, a withered tree opening a crystal site, competitors moving into abandoned regions, a second station on centrality (`@X213`).  ⚠ *"Flavour for players who get invested"* is exact: **only an invested player has accumulated enough state for the interesting conditions to be true** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X217` | ⚠⚠ **a trigger tests WHAT HAPPENED — never a statistic and never a rating** (`BLOCKS.md`: *"a gate that tests capability is a locked door wearing a skill check"*).  ⚠ dryopea already refuses the same thing — `@X131`'s ledger over an affinity bar, `@X207`'s no standings screen — so **triggers read the ledgers this design already keeps**: who you left behind, who you came back for, which trader you dealt straight with, what you sold, where you built.  ⚠ **Every condition must be MACHINE-CHECKABLE**; a trigger needing judgement is not a trigger.  Cheap here, since it is all simulation state | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X218` | ⚠⚠ **NEVER SHOW THE JOIN — and it is what makes OPEN SOURCE SAFE.**  This answers `@X200`'s demand that the game resist optimisation *even by somebody reading the source*, and `BLOCKS.md` supplies the mechanism: ***the state stores the past and never the future; content carries conditions and never a schedule; the engine joins them at play time and DISCARDS THE JOIN***.  ⚠⚠ The player always has **what they did** and **what arrived**, and never **what it enabled** or **why** — so ***reading the content tells you what exists, reading your save tells you what you did, and neither tells you what you have already qualified for***.  ⚠ Absolute prohibition: no *unlocked because*, no requirements display, no notification that a condition was met, **no retrospective**.  To farm an outcome you would need **both halves at once**, and the design never presents the pair | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X219` | ⚠ **two failure modes, and STARVING is the dangerous one** — **flooding** (too much eligible at once; *eligible is not due*, so offer one and leave the rest eligible) and ⚠⚠ **starving** (the player does something nobody anticipated and nothing qualifies).  **The answer is structural: some content must be unconditionally available for ever** — and dryopea already has it, because **the next base always starts** (`DESIGN.md` § 14) and a sortie needs no counterparty, no standing and no history | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X220` | ⚠⚠ ***"Not common" has a RATE, and it decides what may be written: RARE CONTENT MUST BE CHEAP CONTENT.***  Unlikely-to-fire **and** expensive to write is the waste case; unlikely-to-fire and cheap is **depth at almost no cost**.  ⚠ **And the gate should be the player's own progression** — earned rather than withheld, so a player who never sees an event **was never denied anything**, and *never got there* is not *cannot*.  ⚠ It also makes the design extensible for free: a trigger system has no plot to interrupt, so new events are **more conditions to evaluate**.  ⚠⚠ And it stays inside `@X208`'s fence — a trigger system is **reactive, never strategic** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X221` | ⚠⚠ **THE AUTHOR'S SAFETY NET — the design must survive being played by the person who WROTE it** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"I want to play this game myself and I do not want a huge advantage to other players"*).  ⚠⚠ **Strictly harder than `@X200`'s source-reader**, and `@X218`'s *never show the join* **does not help — they wrote the join**.  ⚠ What actually protects the author: **the instance is not the rules** (`@X122` — they know a withered tree opens a crystal site, not **which** tree withered here), ⚠⚠ **other players' actions** (so multiplayer helps the author most), the **compound** of conditions in a live campaign, and weakly, time.  ⚠ **The author will keep some advantage and a design claiming otherwise would be lying** — what this architecture does is make it small and decaying | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X222` | ⚠⚠ **THE AUTHORING TEST: would knowing this event exists change how a player plays?**  **No** → stumble-able, the author can meet it too and a wiki does not spoil it.  **Yes** → a quest with hidden steps, spoiled the moment one person writes it down.  ⚠⚠ **The mechanism: the condition must be a BYPRODUCT of ordinary play, never a checklist** — *you have worked this region for a long time* rather than *visit these five locations*, *you left somebody behind here twice* rather than *sell three pieces of information*.  ⚠⚠ **The rule underneath is that a condition should describe a WAY OF PLAYING, not a list of actions — because you cannot shortcut being a certain kind of player.**  A checklist can be executed by somebody who does not mean it; a playstyle cannot.  ⚠ Follows from `@X217`: a condition made of things-you-did is inherently stumble-able | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X223` | ⚠ **and *stumble-able* should be MOST of them** — some events legitimately want a rare precondition (the deep-lore ones, where § Future contact's no-shortcut rule is the point), but the majority should be the stumble-able kind because those **keep working after the community documents the game**, **let the author play their own game** (`@X221`), and are **cheap**, which `@X220` requires of anything rare.  ⚠⚠ *"Unknown till the moment it happens"* is then **not a promise kept by hiding things — it is what falls out of conditions that describe how somebody played** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X224` | ⚠⚠ **THE SAME SYSTEM BUILDS THE TERRAIN, AND OTHER PLAYERS ARE THE SEED** (owner, 2026-08-26) — *"I do not want to know what to find before I boot up the game"*.  ⚠⚠ **The strongest anti-optimisation statement in the design**: a procedural world has a seed, and if the seed is a number the author chose then **the author knows the world** — but if it is *what other people did*, the author cannot know it, **because it has not happened yet**.  ⚠⚠ **And it is not randomness** (`@X201`): player history has **no expectation to compute** — it is specific, caused and unrepeatable.  ***You cannot precompute a world whose seed is other people's future decisions; the information does not exist yet.***  ⚠ dryopea already does a small version — `@X174`'s abandoned bases, caches and homesteads are terrain nobody authored | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X225` | ⚠⚠ **but the GATES need DETERMINISTIC terrain, and that line must not blur** — 33 `.keys` scripts and 654 measurements play **fixed** maps, and a gate over a generated map measures nothing.  ⚠⚠ ***Hand-authored maps stay, for TESTING.  Generated maps are for PLAY.***  Two purposes, one editor, and the `.keys` corpus is the line between them — which also keeps the editor's reason to exist intact: it is how the bricks get authored and how a fixture gets pinned.  ⚠ And the generator **assembles, never invents** (`../crew_punk/BLOCKS.md`): the biomes and installation types are the **bricks**, conditions and player history the **assembly rule** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X226` | ⚠ **single player still works, because the seed DEGRADES** — without other players it falls back to **your own history** (the per-planet persistence every feature here wants), **NPC operators** (`SETTING.md` § The competitors: *"their tries will be visible on the planet"*), and **the world's own clock** (a tree withering between missions opening a crystal site that was not there).  ⚠⚠ So a long solo campaign still surprises its author, **less than a populated one** — which is exactly why `@X221` says multiplayer helps the author most.  ⚠ And a newcomer is not forced into a veterans' world: the planet is large, unclaimed regions exist, and `@X219` already requires some content be unconditionally available for ever | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X227` | ⚠⚠ **THE POLITICS MUST BE WORTH HAVING FOR SOMEBODY WHO NEVER PLAYS IT** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"I do not like the player politics for myself — I want to experience the **leftovers** of other players, see their struggles and their alliances grow and crumble"*).  ⚠⚠ A different requirement from `@X165`/`@X213`, and **the more important one**: those design politics as something a player DOES, this asks for it as something a player **ENCOUNTERS**, and that audience is larger.  ⚠⚠ **It is the relationship this document already builds with the ROBOT economy** — a world with its own economy you perturb without running — **one layer up, made of people**.  ⚠⚠ **The rule: politics must produce ARTEFACTS, not just state.**  An alliance that is only a database row leaves a stranger nothing; every political outcome must leave **something a stranger can find** — an investment that landed oddly, a second station built by people you never met, a trader who will not deal with you, ⚠ **a shared cache nobody maintains**.  `@X174`'s consequence-made-physical, applied to people | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X228` | ⚠⚠ ***"Grow and crumble"* needs TRAJECTORY, and `@X212`'s DECAY is what supplies it** — you must be able to tell something WAS strong and is now weakening, which reads as **deals that used to happen and no longer do**, a station wing nobody maintains, a trader once central and now quiet.  ⚠ **Without decay a political landscape only accumulates, and nothing that only accumulates can be seen to fall.**  ⚠ It also means the politics must be **SLOW**: fast politics leaves no readable trace, slow politics leaves **ruins**.  ⚠ The channel is the one already built — no standings screen (`@X207`), so a non-participant learns it **by how people treat them and by what the crew mention**: the partial sensors' **fourth** subject | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X229` | ⚠⚠ **OBSERVING is not SPECTATING, and `@X211`'s guard is what makes the difference** — the risk is that a non-participant feels like an audience for somebody else's game, and what prevents it is a rule written for another reason: **the planet is the primary tap**.  A player who ignores the politics entirely still has the whole game, so they are not *watching* while others play — **they are playing their own game in a world that has other people's history in it.**  ⚠ Same difference `DESIGN.md` § 20's abandoned bases already rely on | `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § The capstone |
| `@X230` | ⚠⚠ **PLAYERS ARE IN DIFFERENT CAMPAIGN PHASES, and that is a feature** (owner, 2026-08-26) — a shared world is not synchronised.  ⚠⚠ **The asymmetry is safe because `@X119` keeps the player SMALL**: there is no personal power curve, so a veteran's advantage is **crew skill and equipment, never weapons**.  ⚠ A veteran joining a newcomer's base is **a force multiplier on WORK** — what a struggling base needs — and cannot trivialise the defence, because the layout is still the newcomer's (`@X117`) and **the towers still do the fighting**.  ⚠ So phase asymmetry is **the mentorship co-op gets for free**, and `@X172`'s *let them see what is possible* arriving as a person rather than a ruin | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X231` | ⚠⚠ **end-game players *stir up the meta*, and that is the POINT — with one guard.**  § The end game changes who attacks and makes robots and insects co-belligerents; `SETTING.md` says waking an old one **takes many players**, so an end-game player's actions land on people nowhere near that content.  ⚠⚠ **The guard is `SETTING.md` § Elementals escalate on a different axis — COMMAND, not units** (*"nothing gets stronger, the elementals simply start wanting something"*), generalised into the rule for all end-game spill: ***END-GAME ACTIONS CHANGE WHAT THE WORLD IS, NOT HOW HARD IT IS FOR EVERYBODY.***  ⚠ Factions realign, routes change, new things appear — never a global difficulty multiplier; effects stay **regional** via per-planet persistence; and `@X219`'s *some content unconditionally available* must include **somewhere still ordinary to play** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X232` | ⚠⚠ **the strongest form of `@X224`** — other players seed the world not only with **what they did** but with **WHAT PHASE IT IS IN**, and the phase is something no single player advanced alone, cannot be read off any save, and **did not exist when the author wrote the events**.  ⚠ It also gives a newcomer a reason to care about content they cannot reach: **they see its effects before they see it** — `@X172`'s *it teaches* at the largest scale, and exactly how `SETTING.md` § Future contact says the deep layers should arrive (present as signs long before they are a mechanic) | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X233` | ⚠⚠ **ANTI-ROBOT ALLIANCES CRUMBLE AUTOMATICALLY when a player starts AIDING the robots to line them up against the ancient ones** (owner, 2026-08-26) — the *grow and crumble* mechanism `@X227` asked for, **caused by the campaign's own structure rather than authored**.  ⚠ The chain is all existing design: robots are the mid-game shared enemy → `DESIGN.md` § The end game makes robots and insects **co-belligerents** (humans cannot attack an old one and both other factions inherently can) → so a deep player aids them on purpose → **the alliance's PREMISE has evaporated for that player**.  ⚠⚠ **Nobody betrays anybody: two players, both RIGHT, in conflict** — political tension with **no villain**, arising from `@X230`'s phase asymmetry given a consequence, and needing no diplomacy system, betrayal mechanic or reputation penalty.  ⚠ *"Automatically"* means it is a **trigger** (`@X216`), testing what happened — traffic not attacked, routes left open — never a stat; and it is legible without a UI: you notice **robots stopped dying in somebody's region** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X234` | ⚠⚠ **THE SAME ACT READS TWO WAYS AND THE GAME NEVER DISAMBIGUATES** — aiding robots is also `@X176`'s combative route (pointing the planet at somebody), so from outside ***you cannot tell whether they are helping robots for end-game reasons or to hurt you: the act is identical, only the intent differs, and intent is not visible.***  ⚠⚠ Real political texture **requiring no lying system**, and it is `@X218`'s *never show the join* applied socially — the game presents what happened and never why.  ⚠ It also means **an accusation can be honest and wrong** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X235` | ⚠⚠ **and it is the FIRST SIGN OF THE END GAME for people nowhere near it** — `@X232`'s *effects before content*, made concrete: ***the first evidence of the end game, for a player who has never heard of an old one, is that their ALLY STARTED HELPING THE ENEMY.***  ⚠ Exactly how `SETTING.md` § The old ones says the deep layers must arrive — *present as signs long before they are a mechanic* — with the sign being **another person's behaviour** rather than a portent in the landscape.  ⚠⚠ It also gives the deep lore weight beyond leverage: the truth about the old ones **reorganises who your enemies are**, unevenly, one player at a time | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X236` | ⚠⚠ **NEW PLAYERS KEEP ARRIVING, which is what stops the world CONVERGING** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"there is a natural landscape that will evolve"*).  ⚠⚠ **It names a hazard the rest of § 20 had**: without inflow everyone advances to the end game, everyone ends up aiding the robots, and the mid-game empties — the political landscape collapsing into uniformity, which is *grey soup* at world scale.  ⚠ Continuous arrival maintains a **DISTRIBUTION rather than a march**: somebody is always in their first base, so robots are always a shared enemy for somebody, so anti-robot alliances keep **forming** while `@X233` keeps them **crumbling**.  ⚠⚠ **So the crumbling is TURNOVER, not decay toward nothing** — completing `@X227`'s *grow and crumble* as a **cycle**.  ⚠⚠ **And each phase is CONTENT for the other**: veterans' end-game behaviour is the world newcomers arrive into (`@X235`), and newcomers are who the leftovers are for (`@X227`) — **neither phase waits for the other to finish** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X237` | ⚠⚠ **NOTHING MAY PERMANENTLY CLOSE** — inflow only helps if there is somewhere to arrive **into**, so if a region can be exhausted for ever or a faction destroyed permanently, the world converges anyway and newcomers land in **ruins with nothing in them**.  ⚠ The world must **renew**, and parts already exist: routes reroute when cut (§ 3), trees wither and open shafts between missions (§ The vertical dimension), abandoned bases persist and encroach — ⚠ **the robot economy regrowing what is denied is implied by § 2 and not yet stated, and is worth stating.**  ⚠ `../crew_punk/BLOCKS.md` reaches the same rule from the other side (*"nothing closes — never got there is not the same as cannot"*).  ⚠⚠ **Honest caveat: this assumes real inflow** — a game nobody new joins DOES converge, so the ecosystem's health is ultimately **player numbers**, which is `@X187` exactly: *the dependency is an audience* | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X238` | ⚠⚠ **MOST PLAYERS NEVER REACH THE END GAME, and they must still matter** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"satisfied with playing a couple of scenarios, so they have an influence but do not reach the end game themselves"*).  ⚠ It corrects `@X236`'s shape — **not a pipeline everybody flows along but a distribution heavily weighted early with a thin tail**, which is *better*: the anti-robot layer is permanently and robustly populated.  ⚠⚠ **A short player's contribution is DISPROPORTIONATE because the world KEEPS it** — salvage feeds the market, cut routes perturb the economy, and **bases left behind, caches and stranded crew are content for everybody else for ever** (`@X174`, `@X227`).  ***You do not have to keep playing for your play to keep counting: three bases played and abandoned are three bases in the world.*** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X239` | ⚠⚠ **NOTHING IMPORTANT MAY REQUIRE DEPTH TO INFLUENCE** — if the only levers that matter are end-game ones, the majority of players are decoration.  ⚠ The market must move on **ordinary salvage volume**, not only rare goods; and ⚠⚠ **station investment (`@X213`) must AGGREGATE BREADTH, not only depth** — if centrality is winner-take-all a casual player has none, so **many small positions must be able to outweigh a few deep ones**.  **The politics is a coalition problem, which is what makes it political at all** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X240` | ⚠⚠ **CORRECTS how `@X216` was framed** — *"only an invested player has accumulated enough state"* is true of the DEEP events, and if that is all there is **the event system serves a tiny minority**.  ⚠⚠ ***The distribution of TRIGGER DEPTHS must match the distribution of PLAYERS***: most events fire **early**, on one or two facts in the first few sorties, with compound conditions as the rare tail.  ⚠ `@X220`'s *rare content must be cheap* pointed at the other end — **common content must fire for people who will never come back** — and `BLOCKS.md`'s phase-1 example is that shape exactly (*"they are docked with a cargo.  One fact."*).  ⚠⚠ **And it sharpens the near-term priority: if most players only ever see the early game, the early game IS the product**, so BUILDING being the biggest missing mechanic is the whole point | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X241` | ⚠⚠ **A CAPPED SEMI-OPEN SERVER, AND MANY OF THEM** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ **The cap is not technical — it is what makes the social layer PERCEPTIBLE**: everything in § 20 depends on the world being legible (you notice robots stopped dying somewhere, you hear an outfit lost its berth, you find a cache), and ***among ten thousand players none of that reads — a stranded helper in a crowd is noise, among forty it is a story***.  **Legibility has a population size.**  ⚠⚠ **And many servers resolve the cap against `@X236`'s inflow**: arrivals go to new worlds rather than saturating one.  ⚠⚠ It also gives `@X237` its honest ending — *nothing may permanently close* holds **within a world's life**, but **a world may itself have a life**, and if one runs its course the answer is not a wipe but that others exist.  **The lifecycle is per-WORLD, not per-player** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X242` | ⚠⚠ **THE SERVER BOUNDARY FALLS OUT OF THE PROGRESSION SPLIT: KNOWLEDGE CROSSES, ASSETS DO NOT.**  What the player KNOWS lives in their head and cannot be taken (`@X117`), so it crosses worlds; **crew, standing, carryover and caches do not**.  ⚠ Joining a new world is therefore a genuine restart, and `@X120` already says that is fine — *a returning player skips the ramp with knowledge, not with assets*.  ⚠⚠ **And it protects the new world**: a veteran landing with a deep crew would flatten its early game, which `@X238` says is the only part most players ever see | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
