| `@X243` | ⚠ **a "server" here is a SHARED STORE, not a live host** — worth stating because the word suggests real-time infrastructure this design does not need.  `@X182` established that **every asynchronous feature needs only a shared store**, so **a server is persistent world-state plus identity**; only `@X179`'s team matches want a live host, behind their own trigger.  ⚠ That is what makes *"player groups start their own server"* cheap enough to mean anything | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X244` | ⚠⚠ **SCALE MUST NOT BE ASSUMED — features degrade rather than require population.**  A five-player friends' world and a capped public one are different places, and the load-carriers already exist: **NPC traders** (`@X204`) for a thin market, **NPC competitors** (`@X170`, `@X226`) for no rivals, and `@X229`'s guard — the planet is the primary tap — for no politics at all.  ⚠⚠ **So the political layer must be EMERGENT AND OPTIONAL, never required.**  ⚠ It also completes `@X221`'s safety net: **a world the author does not run, populated by people they do not know, is the strongest version of *other players are the seed*** | `DESIGN.md` § 20 |
| `@X245` | ⚠⚠ **THE FIRST FIFTEEN MINUTES ARE SCOUTING, and the world must be ALIVE** (owner, 2026-08-26) — *"trees, sometimes with visible insects on them, perhaps movement of an elemental, and robots in their normal work flow, busy with whatever project they happen to be in"*.  ⚠ The opening is **LOOKING, not building**.  ⚠⚠ **It needed three things and ALL THREE now exist**: a world to look at (⚠ was *the gap — every robot in the code is a WAVE*, and **BACKLOG B4 shipped it 2026-08-27**, `@X276` — the traffic that was already the waves, given an end and a start), somebody to point at it (was blocked on TEXT, `@X130` — **which this answer promoted from a deferred chore to the gate on the opening, and BACKLOG B1 then SHIPPED**, `@X268`), and somewhere to go (§ X1's rings, which exist).  ⚠ What remains of the opening is CONTENT — a map with roads on it — rather than a mechanic.  ⚠⚠ **So the most valuable unbuilt thing for a new player is AMBIENT LIFE**, and it makes a small piece of `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` an **opening** requirement rather than a Tier E one — not the six installation types, just **a handful of robots doing small work nearby** | `EXPLORATION.md` § X0b |
| `@X246` | ⚠⚠ **THE LANDING ZONE IS NEUTRAL — no big mining, factories or routes there, while they are everywhere else** — a difficulty and legibility gradient built into the **geography**, doing four jobs at once: it gives **a safe place to learn** (which is what makes `@X137`'s no-tutorial ruling affordable — the recon window is unbounded *because nothing near you wants anything*), **small things first** (a lone robot at a small job is legible where a factory's traffic is not), ⚠ **a reason to LEAVE** (the interesting things are elsewhere by construction, so the sortie outward is the geography's own suggestion), and **a mechanical reason the run starts when it does** (`wave_provoke_step` needs a vehicle 12+ hexes out).  ⚠ It is also an **assembly constraint** `@X225`'s generator can hold | `EXPLORATION.md` § X0b |
| `@X247` | ⚠⚠ **WALLS ARE UNCONDITIONAL — *"the helpers are always able to build walls"*** — which makes them the base's **FLOOR**, the same shape as `@X211`'s *the planet is the primary tap* one scale in: **a player who spent badly at landing still has a defence.**  ⚠ That is what lets the landing choice be sharp without being punishing, and `@X238` makes it matter because the early game is what most players ever see.  ⚠⚠ **It also sharpens `@X140` rather than removing it: the risk was never that the player CANNOT build — it is that they never learn they can**, so the fix is **a remark, not a mechanic**.  ⚠ The landing choice itself — **activate the scrambler** (income and pressure at once, since the scrambler IS the core) **or build two towers** (defence with no income) — is the design's own test at minute one.  ⚠ **OPEN: is *or* exclusive?**  Recorded as read; the exclusive version is the better game, because it puts an irreversible decision at the moment the player knows least.  ⚠⚠ **MEASURED 2026-08-28 and the premise does not hold** (`@M065`, `@X288`): the scrambler is the core, a base lands with it ON, and switching it costs nothing — so the two share no scarce thing and there is no `or` to make exclusive.  **The exclusion already exists and is the WALLET's**: 200 points over a 100-point beacon is exactly two, and the third press is refused | `EXPLORATION.md` § X0b |
| `@X248` | ⚠⚠ **THE BRIEFING — a short in-world briefing from the MILITARY about when to be gone, with one or two questions askable** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠ It is how the **PERMIT** arrives (`@X025`), delivered **by the people who enforce it** (*"permit on file = pass; permit missing = destroyed"*), and **a briefing is not a tutorial** (`@X137`) — a character stating a fact about a contract explains nothing about how to play.  ⚠⚠ **It is also the player's FIRST *ask*, which teaches `@X156` by being one** — presence is the interaction, before anything is at stake and with real content rather than an example.  ⚠ *"One or two questions"* is **a budget, not a menu**: the player learns at once that **speech is scarce and worth spending**, which is the expectation `@X154` needs for crew remarks to read as signal later.  ⚠ `@X247`'s landing choice in miniature — a limited irreversible spend at the moment the player knows least | `EXPLORATION.md` § X0b |
| `@X249` | ⚠⚠ **the MILITARY are a deliberately NARROW source** — the sharpest version of `@X150`'s split: they know **the cordon, the permit, the rules and the exit** in authoritative detail, and **not the planet** (they watch from orbit and *"barely intervene on the surface"*).  They carry `SETTING.md`'s truth **2** as their own job and would **deny** truth 3 (`@X155`).  ⚠⚠ So they are **a source who knows a great deal about one thing and nothing about what will actually matter** — characterful, useful, and it quietly tells the player that the people in charge do not understand this place | `EXPLORATION.md` § X0b |
| `@X250` | ⚠ **OPEN — how does the player TRACK the permit?**  `DESIGN.md` § HUD makes the wallet **the only number** and refuses a countdown, so the briefing says *when* and nothing on screen counts down.  ⚠⚠ **Proposal, already written for another reason**: `SETTING.md` § The quarantine puts **two battleships in the sky as ambient atmosphere**, *"slowly traversing the upper view"* — so ***the cordon tells you when to be gone and the cordon is VISIBLE OVERHEAD; the clock and its enforcer are the same object***.  A traversal is a period; where they are is how long you have.  ⚠ Diegetic, no second number, and it makes existing atmosphere load-bearing — the same move `@X098` made with the wallet's colour.  **Owner's to rule on**.  ⚠⚠ **MEASURED 2026-08-28 and the proposal as written is FALSIFIED** (`@M064`, `@X287`): the game's default frame contains **no sky**, missing the horizon by 0.96° — fourteen pixels — so the battleships are never in view unless the player tilts the camera down, and a clock you must tilt to consult is the HUD number this refused, with a tax on top.  Three answers survive and the recommendation is to bring the signal DOWN to the ground as a passing SHADOW | `EXPLORATION.md` § X0b |
| `@X251` | ⚠⚠ **THE OPENING DESCRIPTION IS A TEST, NOT A TASK** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"this is the experience a player has when the game is finished — we are not there yet"*).  ⚠ `@X245`–`@X250` specify the **destination**, and are to be read the way this repo reads its other tests — *does this feature serve the opening described?* — never as a work queue.  ⚠⚠ **Same correction as `@X187` one subject over**: a detailed specification is not a priority signal, and `plans/ROADMAP.md` § The critical path is unchanged with **BUILDING** still the load-bearing gap.  ⚠ The one fact worth carrying forward is small and true: **every robot in the code today is a WAVE**, and there is no mover for a robot going about its business | `EXPLORATION.md` § X0b |
| `@X252` | ⚠⚠ **HELPERS ARE SEMI-AUTOMATIC BY DEFAULT AND SPECIALISED BY ATTENTION** (owner, 2026-08-26) — *"later a player can go to an individual one of them and in that interaction give them specific tasks; from then on they behave less general"*.  ⚠⚠ **It settles a real conflict**: `DESIGN.md` § 11 § Wall paint has helpers picking up build orders themselves, while `@X198` names auto-assignment as the thing that deletes the assignment pillar — and the resolution is that **auto is a DEFAULT rather than a CEILING**.  ⚠⚠ **The pillar survives because DIRECTING SOMEBODY COSTS A TRIP** (`@X156`: presence is the interaction), so **your attention is the scarce resource, not the orders** — *you cannot go to all six, so which one you specialise IS the decision*, which is `@X197`'s *more tasks than helpers* reflected as **more helpers than trips** | `DESIGN.md` § 9 |
| `@X253` | ⚠ **it is a RATCHET, so a crew accumulates SHAPE** — *"from then on"* is persistent and per-helper, so a run produces a crew with a profile: some general, some dedicated.  ⚠⚠ **`@X196`'s clusters applied to BEHAVIOUR rather than skills, and the two reinforce each other for free**: a helper you keep directing to repair *becomes* a repairer under `@X124`'s practice loop, so **direction and advancement point the same way with no mechanism connecting them**.  ⚠⚠ **And over-specialising TELLS YOU**: a general helper is rarely idle while a specialised one is idle whenever their job is not needed where they are, so narrowing somebody makes them **more likely to speak up** (`@X142`) — **the system reports its own over-tuning through the channel that already exists**.  ⚠ **OPEN: can a specialised helper be widened again?**  Natural reading yes; a one-way ratchet would be a very different game.  ⚠⚠ **RULED 2026-08-28 — YES, and it costs a TRIP** (`@X289`): this row's own over-tuning signal is inert without it, `@X255`'s debrief would become a punishment channel, and the SKILL axis widens on its own anyway — **diameter 2, eccentricity 2 for all twelve** (`@M066`).  ⚠ What stays open is whether widening is a full RESET or a STEP | `DESIGN.md` § 9 |
| `@X254` | ⚠⚠ **and it makes the whole assignment pillar DISCOVERABLE rather than mandatory** — a new player never assigns anything and the base still works, with the depth **opt-in by walking over to somebody**.  ⚠ Exactly what `@X137` requires (*a player who never finds this still plays*), and the same shape as `@X247`'s unconditional walls: **a floor that holds while the ceiling is optional** | `DESIGN.md` § 9 |
| `@X255` | ⚠⚠ **ASSIGNMENT IS NEVER MANDATORY — an advanced option, and the WHOLE FIRST SCENARIO plays easily without it** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ **And the debrief is how it is DISCOVERED, which is the half `@X254` was missing**: a helper says they spent the base doing work **beneath what they are good at**.  ***The player is never told the system exists; they are told a PERSON WAS WASTED***, and the rest is theirs to work out.  ⚠ No new channel needed — it is `@X144`'s debrief carrying one more true observation | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f |
| `@X256` | ⚠ **it obeys every rule the debrief already has** — it **points without concluding** (*"I hauled rubble for the whole base"*, never *"assign me to repair next time"*, which `@X149` forbids), it says **nothing false** (`@X148`: the skills genuinely went unused and only the emphasis is theirs), it is **stumble-able** (`@X222`: the condition is a byproduct of ordinary play and cannot be farmed), and **one speaks, never six** (`@X143`).  ⚠⚠ **And the strength of the remark is a PERSONALITY expression rather than a metric** (`@X157`) — a proud specialist minds more than an easy-going generalist.  ⚠⚠ **It must read as a PERSON being underused, never as a SCORE**: the difference is whether the player feels **told off** or feels **they let somebody down**, and only the second produces the curiosity that finds the feature | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f |
| `@X257` | ⚠⚠ **and the same remark is a TUTORIAL the first time and a WARNING the tenth** — being *consistently* wasted is a grievance, `@X131`'s ledger accumulates grievances, and `@X160`'s *"personnel can join competitors when not treated well"* then has a concrete meaning: **ten sorties of being underused IS not being treated well**.  ⚠ One mechanism, two jobs, nothing added | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f |
| `@X258` | ⚠⚠ **THE FIRST TWO HELPERS ARE GIVEN — choice enters at RECRUITING** (owner, 2026-08-26).  ⚠⚠ **It removes the worst instance of the reroll trap: the choice made at MINIMUM KNOWLEDGE** — a template choice is only a decision if you know what the options mean (`@X117`), and before the first sortie the player knows nothing, so offering it there would be **a lookup rather than a decision** and *"the best starting pair"* would be the first thing anybody wrote down.  ⚠ **The fiction supplies the reason, which is better than a design one**: `SETTING.md` § The player is a small mining co-op, so ***you do not choose them because they are who you have*** | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2b |
| `@X259` | ⚠⚠ ***Well balanced* makes them the CONTROL, not a placeholder** — a spread rather than a specialism, so the first scenario plays without understanding specialisation at all (`@X255`), and **they become the baseline the player later notices DIFFERENCE against**.  ⚠⚠ **Which produces an onboarding CURVE with no gates**, each step caused by the previous: given pair plays → recruit **with a sortie's knowledge** → that specialist's skills go underused → they say so at the debrief → the player goes looking and finds assignment.  ⚠⚠ **The order is right by construction**, because the underuse remark is unlikely from a balanced helper and likely from a recruited specialist — *the tutorial arrives exactly when the player has earned the vocabulary to read it* | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2b |
| `@X260` | ⚠⚠ **the default pair are the game's MAIN CHARACTERS** — `@X238` says most players play a couple of scenarios and stop, so **the starting two are the only crew most players will ever meet** and their voices (`@X157`) are the voices of the game.  ⚠ **They are not a placeholder to be filled in later**: they are the performance the majority of the audience sees, and should be authored that way.  ⚠ It holds across worlds too — `@X242` keeps assets off a new server, so **every campaign anywhere opens with these two**.  ⚠⚠ **WRITTEN 2026-08-28** (`@X291`): they are **VESK**, a Spacer engineer on plan/tinker, and **TALLOW**, a Hive-dweller ranger on observe/stamina — `@X196`'s own first two clusters, which are disjoint, whose cores are the two skills C1 built, and which are the two halves of the opening | `PROGRESSION.md` § P2b |
| `@X261` | ⚠⚠ **THE MECHANISM BEHIND `@X137`: A FINE DEFAULT, AND OPTIONS THAT SURFACE WHEN REACHED FOR** (owner, 2026-08-26: *"the game starts with a fine default, and the moment a player wants to tune the situation the options are presented to them naturally"*).  ⚠⚠ ***Every system has a working default, and its controls surface only when the player reaches for them.***  Both halves matter — a **fine** default (not a degraded mode, not a strawman: it genuinely works with no input) and **naturally** surfaced depth (in the world, through channels that already exist — never a popup, an unlock notification or a settings screen).  ⚠⚠ **It is why this replaces a tutorial rather than hiding one**: a tutorial exists because a system does not work until configured, teaches everything at once to somebody who needs none of it, and is *skipped* by the experienced — this teaches **one thing at the moment it becomes relevant** and is **never encountered** by the coping.  ⚠ **Already the house rule in nine places** (`@X252`, `@X258`, `@X247`, `@X254`, § Position triggers, `@X135`, `@X244`, `@X211`, `@X144`) — now with one name | `DESIGN.md` § 11 |
| `@X262` | ⚠⚠ **THE TEST: does this system work with NO input, and does its depth surface WHEN THE PLAYER REACHES FOR IT?**  ⚠ If it must be configured before it works, it needs a tutorial — and there is none, so **it needs redesigning**.  ⚠ Three failure modes: ⚠⚠ **a bad default** (a strawman meant to push the player into configuring — they are then punished for not reading, which is a tutorial with the teaching removed), ⚠⚠ **surfaced too eagerly** (depth offered before it is wanted, a tutorial wearing a different hat — **the trigger must be the player REACHING**, never elapsed time or a counter), and **invisible even when wanted** (a player reaches and finds nothing — which is why `@X255`'s underuse remark exists: **the reach has to have something to meet**).  ⚠ The generalisation of `@X139`'s *can a player find this by playing around?*, moved from **mechanics** to **systems** | `DESIGN.md` § 11 |

### sim — cost and scale ([`plans/22`](../plans/22-the-field-cache/README.md))

| code | decision | owner |
|---|---|---|
| `@X030` | the field is **cached by EPOCH**, and the epoch is bumped **inside** the two mutators (`height_raise` / `height_clear`) — never at a call site | `22-S1` |
| `@X031` | the sweep is **bounded by the ROSTER**, by **PATH** distance never straight-line — the bubble is a sphere and a route can be far longer | `22-S3` |
| `@X032` | **invalidate on EFFECT, not on EVENT** — the field changes only if an edge flipped | `22-S4` |
| `@X033` | ⚠⚠ **simulation granularity must NOT follow the CAMERA.**  If it does, where the player looks changes the outcome.  The boundary is the interaction radii | `plans/22` § What this plan does NOT build |
| `@X034` | **incremental field repair stays deferred** — an incrementally wrong field routes enemies through a wall.  ⚠ Its equality gate is already written and green | `22-S5`, `11-F8` |

### method

| code | decision | owner |
|---|---|---|
| `@X035` | **one caller of `wave_tick`** — asked by COUNT (`play_ticks`) or DURATION (`play_advance`), never one folded into the other | `19-P1`, `@M003` |
| `@X036` | the **mode gates the CLOCK, never the seam** — `in_playing` says what keys mean, `PlayState.playing` says whether time reaches the sim | `19-P3` |
| `@X263` | ⚠⚠ **A `.keys` SCENARIO OPENS AS ITS PREFIX, NOT AS ITS END STATE** — `make play SCRIPT=<name>` truncates the file at its first `tick` / `fall` and plays only what comes before.  ⚠ A scenario's tail is its ANSWER (a fallen base, an exhausted wave list, a wall already broken); what a player wants is its QUESTION — the ground, the walls, the towers, the crew and the armed schedule with the first wave still un-poked.  ⚠ It is also the repo's existing use of the word: `emit.loft::emit_keys` already writes a situation as *"an authored STARTING POSITION with no `tick` in it"*.  ⚠ It **truncates rather than filters**, because a measurement written after a tick is true about the end and false about the beginning.  ⚠⚠ The claim that `tick` and `fall` are the WHOLE advancing set is not left to a comment — the corpus sweep asserts **t0 over every `.keys` file in the tree** | `BACKLOG A1`, `@M044` |
| `@X264` | ⚠⚠ **The launcher's flag is `script=<name>`, NEVER `--script <name>`, and that is loft's rule rather than a style** — the runtime takes a leading `--` argument as one of its OWN and strips it before `arguments()`, so `loft src/main.loft --script x` opens a **MAP** called `x`, silently, looking exactly like a working launch.  ⚠ `loft … -- --script x` does pass it through, but a separator you can forget is the same silent failure behind one more step.  ⚠ The reading lives in `src/scenario.loft` where a test can reach it, never in the entry | `BACKLOG A1`, `@M044` |
| `@X265` | ⚠⚠ **A MAP'S SOURCE IS A `.keys` FILE AND THE `.json` IS BUILT FROM IT — AND BOTH ARE COMMITTED.**  `maps/<n>.json` is ~460 ground entries; a diff over one says nothing a reviewer can act on and nobody can author one by hand, so `maps/<n>.keys` is what a pull request shows: *a wall moved two hexes east*.  ⚠ The JSON is committed anyway because `make play MAP=` loads it and a fresh checkout has no builder run in it.  ⚠ The BUILD saves and the source does not — a `do save` line is ceremony that can be forgotten, and a source that forgot it would report a green build having written nothing; it also keeps the source pure authoring, which is what lets the same file be played directly through `SCRIPT=` (`@X263`).  ⚠ A map holds the GROUND and the MARKERS and **nothing else**: a `crew` or `schedule` line is authored, played and silently dropped, so every shipped map is played solo | `BACKLOG A2`, `@M045` |
| `@X266` | ⚠⚠ **WHAT MAKES A MAP PLAYABLE IS A PURE FUNCTION OF ITS TWO LAYERS, AND THE BUILDER REFUSES A MAP THAT FAILS IT** — `src/maps.loft::map_fault`.  The failure it exists to catch is silent: **a map you can open, drive around and never start a run on**, because no spawn marker is `WAVE_1_PROVOCATION_HEXES` (12) from the core to poke or none of them can walk to it.  `MAP=` loads it, P lands the crew, and the waves never come.  ⚠ Pure of the simulation on purpose — a check that needed a running game could not be asked of a file on disk — so it reads the same whether a map was built from a source, painted in the editor, or written by hand.  ⚠ The gate over `maps/` is a SECOND reading rather than the same one: a map edited in the editor never went past the builder | `BACKLOG A2`, `@M045` |
| `@X267` | ⚠⚠ **THERE IS ONE HUMAN-READABLE CONTROLS LIST AND IT IS GATED AGAINST THE TABLE** — [`docs/PLAYING.md`](PLAYING.md), held to `bindings.loft::editor_actions` by `tests/a4_the_controls.loft`: every action needs a TABLE ROW, and every palette key must be named on the **same row** as the ground it paints.  ⚠ It is not belt-and-braces — `src/main.loft`'s hand-kept copy was **off by one for twenty-five plans**, claiming *"1=sea, 5=grass, 9=wall, 0=wall_high"* when the palette actions are `palette_1`..`palette_11` over entries **0..10**: key `5` paints **sand**, `wall` is on `0`, `wall_high` is on `-`, and `=` is bound to nothing.  Only *1=sea* was right.  ⚠⚠ **A comment is compiled by nothing**, so the second copy is always the one that drifts — the entry's header now POINTS rather than restates.  ⚠ Adding a binding goes red in two places by design: `09_i1` pins the row COUNT and cannot say what the row is, `a4_the_controls` demands it be documented | `BACKLOG A4`, `@M046` |
| `@X268` | ⚠⚠ **TEXT IS REACHABLE HEADLESS, AND THE HALF OF `@X097` THAT SAID OTHERWISE IS STALE.**  BACKLOG B1.  `@X097` refused a text HUD on TWO grounds and only one of them survives: *the HUD is one number* stands (it is `DESIGN.md` § HUD's ruling, not a phase's), while *`draw_text` rasterises through `#native rasterize_text_into`, which answers "native function not loaded" under `loft test`* is **FALSE as measured today** (`@M047`) — it draws 1324 pixels on a headless `Canvas` under the interpreter, with the pinned `graphics` 0.5.2.  ⚠ The binary moved under the claim, which is `TOCHAIN` § The BINARY moves under you arriving at a DESIGN decision rather than at a test count.  ⚠⚠ **The other half was real and is now paid**: `assets/DejaVuSans-Bold.ttf` ships (Bitstream Vera — permissive; Liberation Sans Narrow was refused on LICENCE, being GPL-2-with-exception against this repo's LGPL-3).  ⚠⚠ **The seam is `src/font.loft` and its invariant is that dryopea owns the ABSOLUTE path and loads once**, because a relative path means two different things and which one depends on the `graphics` version — 0.5.2's `gl_load_font` IS the raw native (process cwd), 0.8.0's joins `source_dir()` — so `loft install graphics` would silently stop the font loading.  ⚠⚠ **And silently is the whole problem**: a null handle collapses to **0** at the native boundary, which is a live handle, so a font that failed to load draws *the wrong typeface*, not nothing.  ⚠ `TEXT_FONT_FILE` is the one constant; nothing outside `font.loft` may call `graphics::draw_text`.  ⚠⚠ **It does NOT convert the wallet HUD** — the seven-segment digits are shipped, gated by an independent oracle and sized for legibility in a real window (`@M042`); what changed is that they are now a CHOICE rather than a constraint, and `@X130`'s five blocked features are unblocked | `BACKLOG B1`, `src/font.loft`, `@X097`, `@X130`, `@M047` |
| `@X269` | ⚠⚠ **THE RENDERER NOTICES ANY CHANGE TO THE GROUND IT DRAWS, AND `@X095`'S NARROWER RULE IS RETIRED.**  Plan 27 C2.  `@X095` justified diffing the HEIGHT layer alone with *every terrain change a tick can make moves the height layer* — true while a body raised its hex, a breach raised masonry BEFORE it repainted, and salvage lowered a pile.  **Building falsified it** (`@M048`): a finished order repaints with no rubble, and the watch reported **0** dirty hexes against a tile checksum that had moved.  ⚠⚠ **The replacement is WIDER and needs no claim about what the simulation may do**, which is what made the old one fragile — it was one new mechanic away from being wrong, and building was that mechanic.  `MeshWatch` now snapshots the painted KIND beside the rise, over the union of painted hexes and piles, and reports both directions (a hex painted back to sea and a pile cleared both vanish from the union).  ⚠ The cost is a walk of the painted set per tick rather than of the piles; the paint is ~460 hexes on an authored map against a field rebuild that is ~69% of the suite, and 0 of 654 gate measurements moved.  ⚠ Falsified: delete the painted walk and BOTH the C0 regression and C2's renderer test go red, reading exactly `@M048`'s numbers back | `plans/27` § C2, `play_view.loft`, `@X095`, `@M048`, `@M049` |
| `@X270` | ⚠⚠ **THE WALL TRAIL TOGGLES EVERY HEX THE VEHICLE *ENTERS*, ALONG THE PATH IT ACTUALLY TOOK.**  Plan 27 C3, `DESIGN.md` § 11 § Wall paint.  ⚠⚠ **The path is not a flourish**: at 3 hex/s over a 2/3 s tick the player crosses TWO hexes, so a trail laid at the vehicle's hex alone leaves a gap in every other one — **measured, 6 laid of 13** against 12 with the path — and a wall with holes in it stops nothing.  It walks `lat_line`, which is the very line `drive_along` walked, so the trail cannot disagree with where the vehicle went.  ⚠⚠ **The starting hex is SKIPPED, and that is two bugs at once**: `drive_along` skips it to avoid burning a step, and here the previous tick already ordered it — so laying it again reads as *re-driving* and rubs out the trail's own last link, which is exactly the gappy wall the first version produced.  ⚠ It also makes a PARKED painter harmless, since `lat_line(a, a)` is one hex and the skip leaves nothing.  ⚠⚠ **Two outlines survive a there-and-back drive, and both follow from the rule rather than contradicting it**: the turn-around hex is the return trip's starting hex and is skipped, and the hex driven back to was never ordered on the way out.  ⚠ The trail states NO rules of its own — `order_place` asks `order_fault` and `order_erase` refuses a committed order, so driving over water, a cliff, a heap or a standing wall leaves nothing behind and a hex a helper has started cannot be rubbed out.  ⚠ It lays the LOW wall: `@X139` makes the key table a budget, so choosing the height is a second key and unbuilt work rather than an omission | `plans/27` § C3, `play.loft`, `DESIGN.md` § 11, `@X140` |
| `@X271` | ⚠⚠ **PAINT MODE LIVES ON `PlayState`, NOT ON `WaveState`** — plan 27 C3, the same argument `playing` (`19-P3`) and the camera (`@X014`) make. A second `TowerOrder` would have duplicated the queue, the work loop, the erase rule and the resolve stage to avoid those three.  ⚠⚠ **AND PROBING FOUND A FOURTH BRANCH THAT MATTERS MORE THAN THE OTHER THREE** (`@X274`).  ⚠ `BUILD_GROUND` is **0** so [loft#914]'s silent default lands on the common case.  ⚠ **The marker check is NOT in `order_fault`** — *is there already a marker here* needs the marker world, which a build layer has no business knowing about, so it lives in `spawn.loft::wave_drop`: exactly the split `carry.loft::cargo_destination_ok` makes for a tower TOP, one step further.  ⚠⚠ **`CARGO_BEACON` is the first carry consumer that adds NO row to `cargo_destination_ok` at all** — a beacon's destination needs the palette, the painted world, the standing orders AND the markers, so it can state NEITHER of its destinations there. key table is a budget), the blast damaging the player's own walls (that is `DESIGN.md` § Damage TYPE's cost for splash, and the whole triangle is designed-not-built), and anything drawn in 3-D | | `@X282` | ⚠⚠ **A MOAT IS A TRENCH THE CREW DIG, THE PALETTE'S `drop` IS ITS DEPTH, AND THE DEPTH DECIDES ONE THING ONLY — HOW MUCH IT TAKES TO FILL** — BACKLOG C5, `src/moat.loft`.  `MATERIALS.md` § Defensive structures called it *the cheapest of the three and the one with a mechanism already waiting* — *"a trench plus the drop the palette already has"* — and that held: `order_work_units` gained one NAME, `hex_height` one term, `hex_ground` one threshold, and there is no moat layer, no moat order and no second trail.  ⚠⚠ **A PROBE FALSIFIED THE FEATURE'S HEADLINE BEFORE A LINE OF IT SHIPPED**, which is the half worth keeping: it was designed as *the depth is the COST, because the crew and the player HOVER and fall in* — every water kind carries `walk_vehicle: true` and `drive_along` asks only `can_climb`.  **`walk_vehicle` is read by NOTHING** (`@D006`): `can_climb` refuses a step whose either end fails `hex_walkable`, which answers `walk_ground` for everybody, so the player has never been able to enter water at all and a hover-climb drive stops one hex SHORT of flat sea.  ⚠ So an EMPTY moat is an absolute symmetric barrier at any depth, and the drop's whole job is the WATERLINE: **a pile is a surface only once it clears the water**, so `water`'s 1 m swallows exactly **two bodies** at `BODY_HEIGHT_METRES` — the same *one to two bodies* band `passable.loft` § Why a robot climbs 2.0 m lands on for a ramp onto a `wall`, which is why neither mechanic needed a constant. A branch at `wave_damage` alone would have been dead code, and the work is in the desire SWEEP.  ⚠ What the sweep gained is the rule it always followed said out loud: a WALL is passable there because the wave can break it, and the lifted climb was how that got expressed — a wall's top is walkable and only the climb refused it.  A TRENCH is passable there because the wave can fill it, and water is not a surface at any height, so the NODE rule is what widens.  ⚠ **The SEA is not a moat**, so the field still stops at the coast and `flow.loft` § Three states' unbounded-plane argument is untouched; ROUTING is unchanged, because a routing field that crossed trenches would walk robots into the water.  ⚠⚠ **A TRENCH IS A WALL THAT CANNOT BE UNBRACED**, and that is the rate: `MOAT_FILL_DAMAGE_PER_METRE` is the full `WALL_HP`, the braced figure a wall reaches only closed into a ring, because a hole in the ground has no ends to unzip from — which is what the player buys for the same ten helper-seconds a hex.  ⚠ **Metres per DAMAGE and never per depth**, so the drop stays the timer: `water` costs one regular 150 ticks and a `waterfall` 1200, against a corpus whose longest base is 320, and authored water prices itself out in a measurement rather than in a rule.  ⚠ **ONE door and no per-class table** — a besieger digs with the tool it chews with, so `passable.loft` § ONE AI, per-class DATA is intact.  ⚠⚠ **SPOIL IS NOT SALVAGE** (`@M059`): `loot_rate` pays 0 for it, and the DEFAULT pays, so the row is what stops *letting the enemy fill your trench* being an income stream with no kills in it | `BACKLOG C9`, `moat.loft`, `flow.loft`, `@X282`, `@M059`, `@M060` |
| `@X284` | ⚠⚠ **A WALL IS AS STRONG AS THE GROUND IT WAS CUT FROM, AND THE PALETTE'S `slope` IS THAT NUMBER** — BACKLOG C6, `src/damage.loft` § Footing.  ⚠⚠ **The rock kinds the row asks for DO NOT EXIST**, which is `@X112`'s *check the number exists first* and the C1 `scout` case again: there is no granite, no sand rock and no volcanic rock in `examples/palette.json`.  What there is, is a SLOPE ladder — sand 2, grass 6, hill 12, rock 20, steep_rock 40 — carried since plan 01 and **read by nothing**, exactly as `drop` was before C5.  ⚠ It is the same ladder under another name: a slope is an angle of repose, and an angle of repose is how well a material holds itself up, so this reads the column that exists rather than inventing three that do not.  ⚠⚠ **THE FOOTING IS THE GROUND AROUND THE WALL, NOT UNDER IT, AND PERSISTENCE IS WHAT DECIDED THAT.**  *Under it* is unavailable and `damage.loft` § What the hex becomes has said so since plan 12 B2 — painting `wall` overwrote the hex and `MapFile` cannot grow a second kind per hex ([loft#876] caps the JSON cast at 6 fields).  ⚠ **Nor can a runtime layer hold it**: a wall's strength has to be recoverable from the SAVED world, which holds the painted kinds and the markers and nothing else (BACKLOG B3), or the same wall would have two strengths across a reload.  That leaves exactly two candidates — the wall's own KIND, or its surroundings — and the fiction picks the second, because **the crew do not haul**: `build.loft` has a helper stand at the site and spend seconds, so the stone comes from where they are standing.  ⚠⚠ **The STURDIEST ground in reach wins, and MONOTONICITY is why.**  A mean or a minimum would make a standing wall get WEAKER when the wall beside it broke, because a break paints `BROKEN_GROUND` where a wall used to be — a surprise nobody designed, compounding the unzip `brace_of` already produces.  A maximum cannot fall.  ⚠ The other end of the same property is real and is recorded rather than fixed: a break RAISES its neighbours' footing, so a brittle perimeter stops being brittle where it has already been breached — a self-limiting unzip, and the existing *a broken wall leaves the default ground* simplification showing through a sharper number.  ⚠ **The band is CHOSEN and priced**: `FOOTING_STURDY` 2.0 against the TRENCH (`@M059`), so the best wall and a trench cost the same ten helper-seconds and buy the same clock and the choice between them is about what a barrier DOES; `FOOTING_BRITTLE` 0.5 against one regular's 100 s, so a sand wall is a delay and never a barrier.  ⚠ The palette's top three all reach the ceiling deliberately — past a hill, rock is rock — which is `@X278`'s curve saturating on the EFFECT rather than the level.  ⚠ **Bracing and footing are one idea said twice**: bracing is what holds a wall up SIDEWAYS and moves with the world, footing is what holds it up from BELOW and does not | `BACKLOG C6`, `damage.loft`, `@X112`, `@X278`, `@M050`, `@M059`, `@M061` |
| `@X285` | ⚠⚠ **THE BASE SCALE IS PRIVATE AND `view_ppm(cam)` IS THE ONE DOOR** — BACKLOG C7, `@D002`, `src/editor_view.loft`.  `cam.zoom` had been moved by the wheel, persisted by `save.loft` and reported in `snap`'s state line since plan 01, and **no file that drew anything read it**: the picture was identical at `z1` and at `z6`.  ⚠ The behavioural half is one line — `VIEW_PPM / zoom`, the law the field's own comment already claimed (*1 = closest; higher = more world visible*).  ⚠⚠ **The half that keeps it fixed is making `VIEW_PPM` PRIVATE.**  A test cannot stop the next caller reaching for a base scale that looks like the answer; a constant it cannot NAME can — the same one-door shape as `hex_ground`, `place_marker` and `salvage_at`, and the defect existed precisely because the raw constant was in reach.  ⚠⚠ **AND IT IS NOT ONE PLACE, WHICH THE FIX PLAN GOT WRONG.**  `PROBLEMS.md` said *derive it in the ONE place `render_editor_frame` passes it, so the GL loop and `snap` cannot disagree*; there are **four** paths and the fourth is not a drawing — the GL loop's frame, `snap`'s frame, `classify_world` behind the `frame` measurement, and **`screen_to_hex`**, which inverts a pointer back to a hex.  Deriving inside `render_editor_frame` alone would have made every click land on the wrong hex at any zoom but 1, which is a worse defect than the one being fixed.  ⚠ The rule that falls out: **a scale is only correct if the thing that INVERTS it uses the same number**, and a fix to a projection must always ask what un-projects.  ⚠ The clamp on `zoom` is [loft#914] rather than defensiveness: a partial `EditorCamera { }` literal takes 0, and clamping to `ZOOM_MIN` makes such a camera draw exactly what it drew before this shipped | `BACKLOG C7`, `@D002`, `editor_view.loft`, `@X098` |
| `@X286` | ⚠⚠ **THE CHASSIS FLOATS, AND IT IS ONE RULE WITH TWO DOORS** — BACKLOG C10, `@D006`, `src/passable.loft`.  Every water kind has carried `walk_vehicle: true` since plan 01 and `GROUND_TYPES.md` § The master palette has called *per-agent-type traversal* the design for as long, while **no code in the tree read the column**.  ⚠ `can_travel` is the rule and `can_climb` / `can_hover` are the two ways of asking it, named for their callers — plan 11 F1's *one passability rule, per-class DATA* is what forbids a second traversal, not a second door, and two independent rules is exactly how a mover and a field come to disagree about what ground is.  ⚠⚠ **THE PROBE THAT KILLED C5's HEADLINE WAS RIGHT ABOUT THE CODE AND THE DESIGN WAS RIGHT ABOUT THE GAME** (`@X282`): with the column read, *the depth is the COST because the crew and the player hover and fall in* is simply true — a hovering mover crosses flat sea for free, **drops into a trench for free** because a drop always is, and then owes a climb out that `CLIMB_VEHICLE` (0.4 m) has not and `VEHICLE_BOOST_CLIMB_METRES` (3.0 m) has.  ⚠ So `PLAYING.md`'s *boost is the only way out of a base you have sealed* is true of trenches again, and the palette's 0-1-3-8 is priced against the boost for the first time — `water` and `rapids` are trenches a boost leaves, a `waterfall` is a hole nothing gets out of, which is the same removability ordering `moat.loft` reaches from the FILL side.  ⚠⚠ **`steep_rock.walk_vehicle` GOES FALSE, and that is a correction rather than a workaround.**  The flag stood on *the floating vehicle hovers above terrain* and a 0.4 m clearance does not clear a cliff; it was unearned because the cliff has **no HEIGHT to be stopped by** — `height_override` null, `slope` 40 unread until plan 02 — so `walk_ground: false` was the only thing carrying *this is a cliff*.  Reading the column without it makes a massif drivable, and `maps/the_gap_03.keys` states in its own header that **the gap is the only way through for anybody, in either direction**.  ⚠ It flips back the day a cliff is tall, and the two columns then differ for the four WATER kinds and nothing else — *the vehicle's whole difference from a robot is that it floats on water* | `BACKLOG C10`, `@D006`, `@X282`, `passable.loft`, `@M063` |
| `@X287` | ⚠⚠ **THE BATTLESHIPS CANNOT BE THE CLOCK IN THE VIEW THE GAME SHIPS, AND IT MISSES BY 0.96 OF A DEGREE** — BACKLOG D1, and it is `@X250`'s proposal put to a measurement instead of an argument.  `@X250` reads *the cordon tells you when to be gone and the cordon is VISIBLE OVERHEAD; the clock and its enforcer are the same object*, resting on `SETTING.md` § The quarantine's *"the sky just HAS them, slowly traversing the upper view"*.  ⚠⚠ **The game's default frame contains no sky at all** (`@M064`): the follow camera sits at `CAMERA_FOLLOW_ELEVATION` = 30.96°, its `fov_y` is 60°, so the top edge of the frame is 0.96° BELOW the horizon — **fourteen pixels on a 720-high frame**.  The overview preset at 89° is not close.  ⚠ A player CAN orbit down to see sky (`CAMERA_ELEVATION_MIN` is −89°), and that is the refutation rather than the rescue: **a clock you must tilt the camera to consult is a HUD number with a camera tax**, which is worse than the number `DESIGN.md` § HUD refused.  ⚠ **Three answers survive and they differ materially, so the ruling is the owner's**: (1) **tilt the follow camera** — the horizon enters at exactly 30.0°, so ~25° puts it a tenth of the frame down; ⚠ it changes every frame the game draws and `CAMERA_FOLLOW_ELEVATION` is `atan(3/5)` from a boom plan 21 R2 tuned; (2) **bring the signal DOWN to the ground** — a battleship's SHADOW crossing the base is in the default view, keeps *the clock and its enforcer are the same object*, and adds no number; (3) **drop the overhead reading** and let the briefing's *when* stand alone.  ⚠ The recommendation is (2): it is the only one that costs neither the camera nor the HUD rule.  ⚠⚠ **AND NONE OF THE THREE SHOULD BE BUILT YET** — see `@M064`'s second half | `BACKLOG D1`, `@X250`, `@M064`, `EXPLORATION.md` § X2d |
| `@X288` | ⚠⚠ **THE `or` IS NOT EXCLUSIVE BECAUSE IT IS NOT A CHOICE — THE SCRAMBLER AND THE TOWERS SHARE NO SCARCE THING** — BACKLOG D2, `@X247`, `tests/d2_the_landing_choice.loft`.  The row asks *is "activate the scrambler **or** build two towers" exclusive?* and records the exclusive version as the better game, *because it puts an irreversible decision at the moment the player knows least*.  ⚠⚠ **Measured, the premise does not hold** (`@M065`): the scrambler IS the core (`@X280`), `jammer_new()` returns `off: false` — **a base lands jamming and the player does not switch it on** — and switching it costs **nothing in either direction**.  Spend every point on towers and the scrambler is still on; switch it off and the wallet has not moved.  ⚠ **An `or` requires a shared scarce thing and these two share none**, so there is nothing to make exclusive.  ⚠⚠ **AND THE EXCLUSION `@X247` WANTED ALREADY EXISTS — IT IS THE WALLET'S.**  The opening budget is 200, a beacon is 100, and `beacon_buy` refuses a purchase it cannot cover, so **two is a ceiling the economy enforces rather than a rule anybody wrote** and the third press buys nothing.  `numbers.json` § economy.starting_budget_first_base says the same in its own doc string — *"Lets them order 1 tower + 1 helper before earning loot"* — and *"Failed deposit does not refund"* is what makes the spend irreversible at the moment it is made.  ⚠ So the design's test at minute one is already met, by arithmetic, between the things the wallet buys.  ⚠⚠ **What a ruling would have to be ABOUT, if the owner still wants one**, is the STATION's pre-mission pick (`DESIGN.md` § 16: *"the player picks from inventory which specialised tops to take down"*) — a manifest, gated on carryover and on a station that does not exist.  That is where an exclusive loadout belongs, and it is not a landing rule | `BACKLOG D2`, `@X247`, `@X280`, `@M065` |
| `@X289` | ⚠⚠ **YES — A SPECIALISED HELPER CAN BE WIDENED, AND IT COSTS WHAT NARROWING COST: A TRIP** — BACKLOG D3, `@X253`'s open question.  ⚠ The ruling is forced by three things the design already says, so it is internal consistency rather than taste.  **1. `@X253`'s own over-tuning signal is inert without it.**  It states that narrowing somebody makes them *more likely to speak up*, so **the system reports its own over-tuning through the channel that already exists** — and **a report you cannot act on is a complaint, not a signal**.  That is `@X262`'s third failure mode verbatim: *invisible even when wanted — the reach has to have something to meet*.  A one-way ratchet makes the reach find nothing.  **2. `@X255`'s debrief would become a punishment channel.**  It tells the player *a PERSON WAS WASTED*; under a one-way ratchet that is news of a mistake they can never correct, which turns `@X144`'s discovery channel into the scoring screen `PROGRESSION.md` § P2f refuses.  **3. The SKILL axis already widens on its own, and it is MEASURED** (`@M066`): the lattice has **diameter 2 and eccentricity 2 for all twelve skills**, so a specialist is never more than one statistic from anything they were never asked to do.  ⚠⚠ A one-way BEHAVIOUR ratchet running against a skill layer that generalises regardless would break `@X253`'s own claim that ***direction and advancement point the same way with no mechanism connecting them***.  ⚠ **Symmetry needs no new mechanism**: `@X156` makes presence the interaction, so widening is going to them and saying something different — and the pillar survives because **attention stays scarce** (`@X252`: your attention is the scarce resource, not the orders), so widening one helper is a trip not spent specialising another.  ⚠⚠ **OPEN, and genuinely the owner's: is widening a full RESET or a STEP?**  Recommendation **reset** — a step-wise unwind prices the MISTAKE rather than the ATTENTION, and `@X252` is explicit about which of those is scarce | `BACKLOG D3`, `@X253`, `@X252`, `@X255`, `@X262`, `@M066` |
| `@X290` | ⚠⚠ **CREW MAY NOT BE TRADED — `@X168`'s RESCUE IS THE ONLY WAY A PERSON CROSSES, AND `@X214`'s ESCAPE HATCH IS UNAVAILABLE HERE** — BACKLOG D4, `@X164`'s last open half.  ⚠ **`@X214` resolved the knowledge tension by allowing player→NPC sale only** — *you sold a COPY, not the thing*, so you keep the fact and the knowledge tree became economically live without becoming purchasable.  ⚠⚠ **A person cannot be copied.**  Selling a helper leaves you without them, so the one move that made trading safe for the dominant axis cannot be applied to crew, and what is left is a transfer.  ⚠ **And a crew MARKET is `@X125`'s reroll trap with money instead of dice**: § P2b's whole objection is *paying a resource for the game to hand you an answer* rather than deciding and earning the payoff, and buying until the numbers are right fails § P6a by the identical mechanism — sharpened by `@X162`'s stable prices, which turn a crew market into a pure lookup.  ⚠ **It would also price the courtesy `@X169` needs to be free**: returning a helper is *a favour with NO mechanical reward*, and if crew were sellable then bringing somebody home has an opportunity cost, reputation stops being free, and **abandonment becomes a way to supply the market** — which inverts *the default must be RETURN, or multiplayer becomes a punishment*.  ⚠ What a buyer would be buying is **another player's ATTENTION** (`@X124`'s practice loop, `@X252`'s scarce resource), which is the category `@X164` forbids one axis over.  ⚠⚠ **The version that survives is the one `@X168` already describes: the PERSON chooses, never the employers.**  `@X160`'s dismissed helper going to a competitor is that event today; a helper leaving one player for another is the same event with two names on it, and it is a choice out of `@X131`'s ledger rather than a transaction | `BACKLOG D4`, `@X164`, `@X168`, `@X214`, `@X125`, `@X169`, `@M067` |
| `@X291` | ⚠⚠ **THE STARTING PAIR ARE VESK AND TALLOW, AND `@X196` ALREADY CHOSE THEM** — BACKLOG D5, `PROGRESSION.md` § P2b § WHO THEY ARE.  `@X260` makes the default two the game's main characters — *the only crew most players will ever meet* — and says they are **not a placeholder to be filled in later**; `@X258` calls them *the default (and well balanced) set*.  ⚠⚠ **Almost all of it is DERIVED, and the one authored part says so.**  `@X196` names the clusters outright — *plan/tinker is the ENGINEER, observe/stamina the FIELD HAND, charisma/agility the nimble talker* — and the three are **pairwise disjoint** (six statistics, three pairs), so *well balanced* has an exact reading: two crew whose clusters share no statistic **cannot converge under `@X124`'s spill and cannot be blind in the same direction**, which is what `@X129`'s partial sensors requires (gated, `@M066`'s file).  ⚠⚠ **The third cluster is excluded on MECHANICAL grounds rather than taste**: the talker's core skill is `stealth` and the game has no number for it, while the engineer's core is `repair` and the field hand's is `scout` — **both among the three BACKLOG C1 actually built**, which no other pairing can claim.  ⚠ They are also the two halves of the opening (§ X0b's scouting, `@X247`'s unconditional walls), and **both are off-planet people because `@X150` requires it**: `SETTING.md` § The recruitment has Centaur Mining doing automated asteroid mining on a contract they have no experience of, so a colonist would quietly break the rule that the crew know no more about Dryopea than the player.  The two backgrounds are `@X152`'s own worked example (*a hive dweller and a spacer are worth being different people*) and the classes come from `archive/gameplay.data`'s list.  ⚠⚠ **VESK** — Spacer, Engineer, plan/tinker: notices what a thing will take, blind to anything moving, speaks in quantities and never exclaims, and **is `@X140`'s fix** — the wall-paint toggle's discoverability risk, answered by *a remark, not a mechanic*.  ⚠⚠ **TALLOW** — Hive dweller, Ranger, observe/stamina: notices movement and ground, blind to whether it holds, and speaks in **terse present-tense position reports**, the voice `@X157` names for a scout outright.  ⚠ **The VOICES are the authored part and the owner may recast them**; everything else is forced | `BACKLOG D5`, `@X260`, `@X258`, `@X196`, `@X157`, `@X152`, `@X150`, `@M066` |
| `@X292` | ⚠⚠ **THE DESIGN'S ANSWER IS THE RULE; A CURRENT IMPLEMENTATION IS A STEPSTONE AND MUST NEVER BE WRITTEN DOWN AS ONE** (owner, 2026-08-28: *"the actual game will have the design ending, every other implementation is just a stepstone to the full one.  So never write the current implementation one as a rule, it can exist for the time being to get written out before the game is done"*).  ⚠ **The ruling that prompted it**: does `wallet_broke` survive the scramble?  **No** — `DESIGN.md` § 14 is categorical (*dryopea does not have a fail screen… every base ends with the player launching the rocket*) and § 4 makes the core invulnerable precisely so *the player is never forced out*.  The wallet reaching zero is **poverty, not an ending**.  ⚠⚠ **But the general half is the important one.**  dryopea documents in RULES with warnings, and a rule that describes today's code acquires the authority of a design decision — so the next contributor defends the stepstone instead of replacing it.  `wallet_broke` had become *"the run's ONLY end state"* in `CLAUDE.md`, in `wallet.loft`'s own header and in four `@M` rows, and every scenario's prose says *the base FELL*, which is the stepstone's framing asserted as fact.  ⚠ **How to write it instead**: say what the DESIGN's answer is and mark the current behaviour temporary — *today X; the design says Y and X goes* — never as an invariant, never as the base of a hard-won rule, and never in a measurement's prose.  ⚠⚠ **It is the counterweight to this repo's greatest strength**: writing everything down as a warned rule is what makes dryopea navigable, and it is exactly why a stepstone written that way is dangerous | `DESIGN.md` § 14, `plans/28`, `@X293` |
| `@X293` | ⚠⚠ **THE BASE NEVER ENDS ITSELF — STAYING IS A LEGITIMATE USE OF THE GAME** (owner, 2026-08-28: *"the player can have their own reason to wait out their time.  They might be a spectator to something else that is going on.  **It is always their decision to stop.**"*).  ⚠ It is the sharp edge of `@X292` and of `DESIGN.md` § 14's *no fail screen*: not merely *the player is not forced out by damage*, but **there is no state at all in which the game takes the decision away**.  ⚠⚠ **So every candidate ending is refused by name**: the wallet at zero is poverty and the base plays on, earning nothing; the wave list running out is `DESIGN.md`'s *free scramble PHASE* and not a finish; an expired permit costs the CARGO and not the launch (`EXPLORATION.md` § X2d, already written this way); and even a destroyed vehicle only respawns at the core and arms a countdown **that exiting cancels** — the one place the game comes closest to deciding, and it hands it straight back.  ⚠⚠ **AND IT MAKES THE WORLD LOAD-BEARING IN A WAY NO MECHANIC DOES**: *a spectator to something else that is going on* means the base is a place worth simply REMAINING in, which is what `@X245`'s living world and `errand.loft`'s ambient traffic (BACKLOG B4) are FOR — they stop being scenery the moment staying is a choice the player may make for no mechanical reason at all.  ⚠ It also quietly forbids a whole class of feature: anything that hurries the player along — an auto-launch, a hard timer, a *"nothing left to do"* prompt — is the game making the decision, however gently | `DESIGN.md` § 14, `@X292`, `plans/28`, `@X245` |
| `@X294` | ⚠⚠ **BESIDE THE CORE YOU TRADE; ON THE CORE YOU LEAVE** — `plans/28` S1, `src/scramble.loft`.  `DESIGN.md` § 4 has a 7-hex prism with six faces and an *opening* the vehicle drives **through** into an interior, and `plans/28` § S0 measured that **none of it exists**: the core is ONE marker on ONE hex of ordinary ground, and a drive straight across it does not notice — **a marker is not terrain**.  ⚠ So the position had to be chosen, and the one that earns its place is **the core's own centre hex**, because it is distinguishable from *within `CARGO_REACH_HEXES`*, where `beacon_buy` debits the wallet and `wave_drop` delivers a top.  Two core interactions, two positions, no new geometry.  ⚠⚠ **And `vehicle_respawn` already puts the player there**, so § 4's *"vehicle death starts the launch countdown automatically — a free 'ready to leave?' prompt"* falls out with **no code at all**, which is the strongest evidence the hex is right.  ⚠ The CREW's rule is deliberately wider — the core's own 7-hex footprint (`numbers.json` § core.footprint_layout, `hex_disc_radius_1`) — because the player drives into the interior while a crew member merely has to have reached the rocket; the generosity is the honest proxy for § 4's *helpers board on their own*, which is not built.  ⚠ Exiting CANCELS and cancelling RESETS, which is § 4's own word: there is no banked progress, so stepping out at five seconds and back in starts from six | `plans/28`, `@X293`, `DESIGN.md` § 4 |
| `@X295` | ⚠⚠ **THE SEMI-AUTOMATIC DEFAULT IS MYOPIC ON PURPOSE, AND THE RADIUS IS THE CREW MEMBER'S ALONE** — `plans/29` O1, `src/task.loft`.  `DESIGN.md` § 9 § SEMI-AUTOMATIC BY DEFAULT says helpers *"find work, and a new player never has to assign anything"*, and `@X262` makes *does this work with NO input?* the test every system here has to pass.  ⚠ Until O1 nothing in `src/` moved a crew member at all: `helper_drive`'s only caller was the `.keys` script runner, so a crew member stood wherever a fixture left them for the whole run and the four jobs they can do each reach exactly ONE hex (`plans/29` § O0).  ⚠⚠ **The size of the radius is what decides whether `@X252` holds.**  It resolves `@X198`'s refusal of auto-assignment by making auto *a DEFAULT rather than a CEILING*, and that only works if the default is genuinely fine AND genuinely not optimal.  ⚠⚠ **The first version asked the full `detect_sees`, so a heap's own `notice_of_heap` widened the work radius from 3 hexes to 6 — and it moved 18 tests across 8 files, all one way**: bases that used to fall stood for 800 ticks with 0 enemies alive, and a wallet that ended at 40 ended at 299 of 300.  ⚠ **The reason it was wrong is not the size**: `@X277`'s two radii answer *is this thing worth driving to*, which is the PLAYER's question and the one `MATERIALS.md` § The governing rule needs; this asks *what is under my nose*, which is a fact about the person and nothing about the pile — a work radius that grew with the depth of a heap would make a crew member's attention a property of the rubbish.  ⚠⚠ **And `@X198`'s own test is what it failed** — *does this make ONE axis dominate?*  At six hexes the default absorbed the work `DESIGN.md` § 9 § The scarcity is STRUCTURAL says growth is supposed to CREATE: towers refilled themselves and bodies cleared themselves, and **a default that absorbs the work it creates deletes the table the pillar stands on**.  ⚠ So the rule is `lat_distance <= detect_radius(sk)` — 3 hexes untrained, 4 at scout 5, 5 at scout 10 — and the SCOUT skill widening it for free is `@X253`'s *direction and advancement point the same way* arriving with no mechanism connecting them | `plans/29`, `@X252`, `@X198`, `@X262`, `@X277`, `DESIGN.md` § 9 |
| `@X296` | ⚠⚠ **AN ORDER IS AN ORDER — `helper_drive` IS NOT OVERRULED BY THE SEARCH** — `plans/29` O1, `src/helper.loft`.  The first version of `wave_assign` rewrote every crew member's destination on every tick, and **the corpus is what said so**: `tests/17_t3` drove a crew member at the gate every tick of the run and it walked off to a heap, while a `.keys` file's `send 0 8 1` became a line the simulation ignores.  ⚠ **It is not a concession to the corpus.**  A verb that says *go here* has to be honoured or the vocabulary lies, and `plans/18` § S1a's *a script AUTHORS and never simulates* is the rule it was breaking.  ⚠ So there are TWO doors onto one pair of fields — `helper_drive` is somebody else's decision and `helper_seek` is the crew member's own — and only the first survives the next tick's search.  ⚠ `helper_direct` clears it: **one order at a time**, so a remit replaces a hex somebody was sent to and cycling round to `TASK_ANY` hands them back to the default completely.  ⚠⚠ **And it is invisible to the game as it ships**: no key sends a crew member to a hex, so `ordered` is never true in a played session and every crew member is semi-automatic — which is what keeps `@X262`'s *works with NO input* true while the script layer keeps its authority | `plans/29`, `@X295`, `plans/18` § S1a |
| `@X297` | ⚠⚠ **THE REMIT TRADES BREADTH FOR REACH — one key, a CYCLE, and the widening is a RESET** — `plans/29` O3, `src/task.loft`.  `@X252` says the player *"can go to an individual one of them and in that interaction give them specific tasks instead — from then on they behave less general"*, and the shape it takes is: **undirected is all four jobs inside their own senses; directed is ONE kind across the whole map.**  ⚠⚠ **Both halves are load-bearing.**  A remit that only NARROWED would be a pure loss nobody would ever give — `@X253`'s payoff (*a helper you keep directing to repair becomes a repairer*) needs `@X124`'s practice loop, and `@M066` measured that nine of the twelve skills have no number in the tree at all.  One that only WIDENED would be free, and a decision that costs nothing is not one.  ⚠ It keeps `@X198`'s axes incommensurable: directing buys global coverage of ONE currency at the price of local coverage of all four.  ⚠⚠ **The key is `G` and it CYCLES, which answers `@X289`'s open half by construction**: widening is the same interaction as narrowing, so a player who has narrowed somebody too far drives back out and keeps pressing until they come round to `any`.  ⚠ **That is the RESET reading rather than the STEP one**, and it is `@X289`'s own recommendation for `@X252`'s reason — *the presses are free and the TRIP is what is scarce*, so a cycle prices the attention and a step-wise unwind would price the mistake.  ⚠ `@X139` makes the key table a budget and asks for an argument; there are two, and the second decides the shape — directing **cannot be a POSITION** (driving past somebody would set their remit every frame and there would be no press left that means *general*), and `play_direct_crew` keeps the position half so pressing `G` in an empty field does nothing at all | `plans/29`, `@X252`, `@X253`, `@X289`, `@X198`, `@X139`, `@X156` |
| `@X298` | ⚠⚠ **THE ECONOMY RUNS ON THE SERVER AT 1.5 km HEXES, AND A SCENARIO IS A FROZEN SNAPSHOT OF ONE CELL** (owner, 2026-08-28) — *"the economy simulation is inactive on this scale, but runs on the common server where each scenario is a snapshot fed from the current state"*, and *"the whole economy is less detailed than each scenario, it holds a far coarser map (1.5 km hexes) with economic activity mapped to that"*.  ⚠⚠ **The ratio is the architecture**: dryopea's `HEX_DIAMETER` is 1.5 m, so an economy cell is **1000× linear and a MILLION× by area**, and a 120-hex-wide scenario is 156 m — **10.4 % of one cell across, about 1 % of it by area**.  ⚠ Three things fall out of the arithmetic rather than being chosen: **a node is almost never on the map** (a 156 m base lands on a mine sited somewhere in 1.5 km about one time in a hundred), so a node on a scenario map is a DELIBERATE sortie-selection choice; **what a base gets is ROUTES, and they cross** — 1.5 km of route against a tenth of it covered, which is `SETTING.md` § They were on an ERRAND made literal, *the player's base is an accident on somebody's commute*, with both ends off the map; and **the 25-hex bubble is 32.5 m, 2.2 % of a cell**, so where you land INSIDE the cell decides whether it touches a road at all.  ⚠⚠ **It SUPERSEDES `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § What this design does NOT do's *no economy simulation*** — the simulation exists, it is somewhere else, which is a division of labour rather than a refusal.  ⚠ `@X243` already makes a server *a persistent world-state store plus identity* and not a live host, so the advance is LAZY: applied when a sortie reads the cell, from the time since it was last touched.  ⚠ And a sortie hands back a DELTA, which is the only thing that makes the player's interference matter | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md`, `@X243`, `@X177` |
| `@X299` | ⚠⚠ **A MOB HAS A RULE RATHER THAN A STATE, SO AN UN-TRACKED ONE IS COMPUTED — NOT SIMULATED COARSELY** (owner, 2026-08-28) — *"the scenario can become bigger via player movement, but the radius where each mob should be tracked is quite shorter than that … because most mobs have a rule instead of a state during the scenario"*.  ⚠ The first half alone would be exactly the simulation LOD [`plans/22`](../plans/22-the-field-cache/README.md) refuses, and the refusal is sound: *LOD's gate is 'the outcome is unchanged', which is a much weaker statement that has to be defended per interaction*, plus *granularity must NOT follow the camera*.  ⚠⚠ **The second half removes the thing the refusal is about.**  A mob's destination is a pure function of five fixed anchors and the world, so an un-tracked mob is not approximated — **it is not simulated at all, because its answer is computable**, and materialising at radius `R` or `2R` gives the identical hex.  ⚠ **So the gate is EQUALITY again**, the same shape `11_f8::test_the_field_a_tick_uses_equals_a_fresh_build` already has, and *granularity must not follow the camera* holds VACUOUSLY — the rule follows nothing.  ⚠⚠ **A mob is a RULE until something makes it a STATE, and dryopea already owns the thing that makes one**: the bubble cuts a robot's link irreversibly, so the tracked set is *everything the bubble has taken plus everything inside an interaction radius* — and the radii are the list plan 22 already gives (tower 15, bubble 25, nibble 1, salvage 1, blocker = same hex), measured from the CORE and the PLAYER and never the camera.  ⚠ **Deviation is a property of the materialised BODY**, so it exists only inside the radius — which is the owner's own *in the action that is visible to a player* — and it must not change where a mob ENDS UP, only how it got there | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `plans/22`, `@X298` |
| `@X300` | ⚠⚠ **THE RULE MUST BE BOUNDABLE, NOT MERELY EVALUABLE** (owner, 2026-08-28) — *"that is the reason why crawler has this AI model — in a given area there are far less mobs than there are on the complete map.  But that is fine as long as the overall mobs have a rule that can be **inspected to see if they can be within a window**"*.  ⚠⚠ **Stronger than *the rule can be evaluated*, and that is the point**: a rule you can only evaluate costs one evaluation per mob per tick over the WHOLE map, which is the linear scan `crawler` measured (`near_mobs_test.loft`: a spatial index gives 22× fewer candidates, **344 ms → 39 ms**, identical near-sets) and did not adopt.  A rule you can BOUND costs a query.  ⚠ So a role answers two questions — **bound** (*could this mob EVER be in this window?*, static, asked once at spawn) and **evaluate** (*where is it now?*, per tick, per candidate) — and three tiers follow: CULL against the static bound (a mob skipped **for the sortie**, not for the tick), EVALUATE the survivors, MATERIALISE only what is inside.  ⚠⚠ **The bound is static because the ANCHORS are** — fixed when the mob enters the patch and never changed, the same property `crawler` sizes its field cache from — so a mob's reachable region is the corridor hull of three known hexes and can be indexed ONCE.  ⚠⚠ **A role whose route cannot be bounded cheaply BREAKS the model**, and that is the test for a new one: *wander at random* has no bound, *patrol between two posts* has an exact one.  ⚠⚠ **And against the BUBBLE the cull decides the sortie**: plan 22 establishes that the bubble is centred on the core and never moves, so *does this route ever get cut off?* is answerable **the moment the base lands** — which turns `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md`'s *"the only thing that matters is which edges the base happens to sit on"* into a computable predicate, and means a sortie knows its own wave pressure before a robot has walked anywhere | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `@X299`, `plans/22`, `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` |
| `@X301` | ⚠⚠ **A SCENARIO CARRIES POINTS OF INTEREST, AND A POI OWNS ITS MOBS** (owner, 2026-08-28) — *"a given scenario has a set of points of interest and these have their attached mobs to them with their cycle that can be quite far and elaborate"*.  ⚠⚠ **It is the layer `@X298`'s ratio was missing, and it CORRECTS the reading of it**: the economy's node is a 1.5 km CELL FACT (*this cell mines*), while a point of interest is its **local expression on a patch** — the working face, the spoil heap, the maintenance shed, the nest, the wreck field.  So *an economy node is almost never AT a base* stays true and *nothing to look at* does not: **a base lands in a neighbourhood and the neighbourhood has features**, several of them, at scenario scale.  ⚠ A mob is therefore not spawned onto the map and given a job — **a POI has a population and each of them has a cycle anchored on it**, which may run far off the patch and back, because off the patch is simply un-tracked (`@X299`).  ⚠⚠ **And it collapses `@X300`'s bound from per-mob to per-POI**: if every mob is anchored on a POI then **the POI IS the bound and culling one culls its whole population** — one query instead of `N`, over an index that is STATIC for the sortie where positions are not.  ⚠ It also makes a scenario authorable in one step (place the POIs, attach the populations) and is what a `.keys` file should carry rather than a mob list.  ⚠ **And it is where a distraction gets its reason**: § Distraction needs the mob to leave its route for something the player BUILT, and the POI is the other half — the thing it was doing it FOR | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `@X298`, `@X300` |
| `@X302` | ⚠⚠ **AN ELABORATE CYCLE MUST STAY CLOSED-FORM — EVALUABLE AT AN ARBITRARY `t` IN O(LEGS), NEVER BY STEPPING FORWARD** (owner, 2026-08-28: *"but that should not make the calculations impossible to perform for a given window"*).  ⚠ It is the hard constraint on how elaborate `@X301`'s cycles may BE, and a cycle of legs gives it for free: one modulo for the phase, O(legs) to find the leg, one interpolation — so *elaborate* costs **legs**, legs are a handful, and the query is as cheap at hour two as at minute one.  ⚠⚠ **What destroys it is anything PATH-DEPENDENT, and deviation is the thing that tries**: a mob delayed or pushed off its line has a HISTORY and history has no closed form.  ⚠⚠ **The resolution is ONE INTEGER — `slip`** — so `position(t) = cycle(t - slip)`: a deviation makes the mob genuinely LATER rather than somewhere else, the closed form survives as the same function with a shifted argument, and **the BOUND is untouched** because the region a cycle covers does not depend on when it is traversed.  ⚠ That last property is what keeps the cull cheap in the presence of deviation at all, and it makes a sidestep COST something instead of being free.  ⚠⚠ **THE RULE IS THE GROUND TRUTH AND THE BODY IS THE APPROXIMATION**, never the reverse — a materialised mob re-converges on the rule, and one that CANNOT (cut off, wrecked, walled in) has taken `@X299`'s one-way door into being tracked, which is `wave_cutoff` and has existed since BACKLOG B4.  ⚠ **The budget test to gate on**: O(POIs) to cull, plus O(legs) per survivor, plus the tracked roster dryopea already pays for | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `@X299`, `@X300`, `@X301` |
| `@X303` | ⚠⚠ **THE TEST FOR EVERY MOB-ROUTINE DECISION: does this make behaviour more BELIEVABLE, or does it only simulate MORE?** (owner, 2026-08-28) — *"this is all for 2 concrete reasons: do not simulate a world for the scenario, but get realistic routes that make the mobs have a believable behaviour."*  ⚠⚠ **The pair is the problem, because either half alone is easy**: a world simulation buys believability at a cost this game cannot pay, and a cheap fake buys the saving and no believability.  Every mechanism in [`ERRANDS.md`](../docs/ERRANDS.md) — the rule instead of a state (`@X299`), the bound (`@X300`), the closed form and `slip` (`@X302`) — exists to get the second without the first.  ⚠ **Worked verdicts, because a test is only useful if it decides cases**: node stock ticking during a sortie ❌ (nobody can see a number `DESIGN.md` § HUD will not put on screen); a hauler carrying something VISIBLE ✅ (it is `@X053`'s harvester body, already drawn); moods, needs and memory ❌ (and `@X131` refuses the bar one system over); a robot walking ROUND a rock ✅ (`crawler` measured the ugly version — greedy *"walked into the first concave obstacle and stopped there — permanently"*); a robot going HOME at the end of its round ✅ (it replaces a deletion).  ⚠⚠ **And the line that makes the rule/state split principled rather than an optimisation: BELIEVABILITY is owed where it can be OBSERVED, CONSISTENCY is owed everywhere.**  Inside the tracked window a mob owes a believable PATH — round obstacles, no teleporting, no standing in a doorway for the run — which is why deviation exists only there; outside it nobody observes anything, so what is owed is that the rule never produce a contradiction the player can catch when they next look.  ⚠ **So the cheap half is not a cheat**: it is not a lower-fidelity simulation of the same thing, it is the same answer COMPUTED.  ⚠ It also decides AUTHORING — detail belongs on the legs a player will watch — and that is deliberately a guideline and not a runtime rule, because a runtime rule varying with what the player can see is what `plans/22` refuses | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `@X299`, `@X300`, `@X302`, `plans/22` |
| `@X304` | ⚠⚠ **A POI IS NEVER CULLED, A BROKEN ONE IS NOT AN ABSENCE, AND STATE BELONGS TO THE PLACE RATHER THAN TO THE TRAVELLER** (owner, 2026-08-28) — *"the POI should not be culled during a scenario, even when a mine collapses the workers will still move towards it and be witness that it is now impossible to work there and see if they can repair it."*  ⚠ **It corrects a word `@X300` was using for two things**: *not MATERIALISED* (outside the window, no body drawn, but it is still there and its cycles still run) is always allowed and costs nothing, while *CULLED* — removed from the scenario with its population — is **never** allowed.  ⚠⚠ **A collapsed mine is a STATE of the POI, not the end of it**, and the workers still come: they walk to a face that is no longer there, arrive, and find out — which is the raid's consequence arriving where the player can watch it.  ⚠ **It is the fiction's own behaviour**: `SETTING.md` § They approach to REPAIR has the swarm reading any broken thing as *"they try to get to the source of the disruption to see what they can do about it"*, so a stream of confused workers milling at a wrecked face is exactly what these robots do — and it is the most believable thing in `ERRANDS.md` by `@X303`'s test.  ⚠ Three mechanics follow: **a raid is not a permanent free win** (the swarm answers a broken thing by sending repair capability, `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § 5 and `DESIGN.md` § Boss = mobile REPAIR PLATFORM — **collapsing a mine may SUMMON the machine that fixes it**, which is § 20's *nothing may permanently close* at scenario scale); the sortie's delta gains a second column (*what the world had started to repair*); and a POI has a small state set a mob can SEE on arrival.  ⚠⚠ **And the division is what saves `@X302`**: a POI may hold state because there are few of them and they do not move, a MOB may not because there are many and they are everywhere — so `position(t) = cycle(poi.state, t - slip)` is **still O(legs) and the BOUND is unchanged**, every cycle a POI issues being anchored on it.  A state change is a rare timestamped event, so the form is PIECEWISE closed: one extra field on a POI and nothing on a mob.  ⚠ *Witnessing* costs no mechanism — what a mob does on arrival is a LEG selected by the state | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `@X300`, `@X301`, `@X302`, `@X303`, `SETTING.md` |
| `@X305` | ⚠⚠ **FEW POINTS OF INTEREST, AND EACH ONE LOAD-BEARING — TWO TO FOUR, AND A POI EARNS ITS PLACE IF REMOVING IT MOVES THE CLOCK** (owner, 2026-08-28) — *"there should be a limited set of POIs to prevent overload for players.  But the ones who are there should have a profound impact on the scenario."*  ⚠ **A BUDGET, and the same one `@X139` puts on the key table** (*a new row needs an argument*) and `DESIGN.md` § HUD keeps with one number on screen; dryopea's sets are all small on purpose — four marker kinds, four robot roles, six helpers, eleven palette hotkeys.  ⚠⚠ **The COUNT is derived rather than chosen**: dealing with a POI is a TRIP, a sortie affords a handful, and `@X197` wants always more tasks than there is time for — so **the count should exceed what one sortie can act on and not by much**.  Fewer than two and there is nothing to choose between; more than about four and the ones never reached stop being a decision and become noise.  It is the assignment pillar one layer up: *which one you go to IS the decision*.  ⚠⚠ **And *profound impact* is stated in dryopea's own currency, or it is a wish**: every mechanic here carries a measured worth — a wall **+44** (`@M050`), a trench **130/174/221** (`@M059`), a re-armed plate **+106** (`@M057`), an order **+34** (`@M070`) — so **a POI earns its place if REMOVING IT MOVES THE SCENARIO'S CLOCK, measured as a scenario pair one token apart.**  ⚠ A POI that changes the picture and not the play is SCENERY, and scenery does not get a population — which is not a slur, it is a different budget: a wreck field with nothing attached is worth painting and simply is not a POI.  ⚠ Three ways one can pay, and a good one pays twice: **pressure** (a route from it crosses your bubble, so it IS your wave), **a parameter** (a maintenance point in reach refunds chip damage, making *kill* and *hurt* different verbs), **a target** (raiding it changes the run, and the swarm answers — `@X304`).  ⚠⚠ **And the limit is what makes the rest affordable**: `@X304`'s *never culled* costs nothing for a handful of places, and `@X300` indexes bounds PER POI, so a scenario with four has four bounds to test rather than four hundred | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `@X139`, `@X197`, `@X300`, `@X301`, `@X304` |
| `@X306` | ⚠⚠ **A FINISHED SCENARIO HANDS THE SERVER A COMPACT RESULT, AND THE RESULT IS THE SNAPSHOT CHANGED — NOT A SECOND FORMAT** (owner, 2026-08-28: *"the server should get a compact result of finished scenarios to use as a basis for future missions"*).  ⚠⚠ **Half of it is already BUILT**: `plans/28` S3 shipped `scramble.loft::manifest_of` — `Manifest { points, crew, left }`, read at liftoff, gated by `tests/28_s3` and measured by `@M068` (200.0 cut short against 225.3 played out) — and plan 28 closes on the exact sentence this answers: *"carryover is produced and measured; nothing consumes it."*  ⚠ `@X298` has a sortie READ one coarse cell, so the result is the same rows written back: **the rate the player DENIED per crossing, the state each POI was left in (`@X304`), plus the player's Manifest**.  ⚠⚠ **There is nothing to design a format for**, and a result with its own schema would be a second description of the world that drifts from the first — the mistake `damage.loft` § What the hex becomes and `compare.loft`'s hand-maintained field list each cost dryopea once.  ⚠⚠ **TWO destinations that must not be confused**: the `Manifest` is the PLAYER's (`@X188`'s per-planet-per-player path, § 14's 1:1 carryover), the cell delta is the WORLD's and shared — because `@X177` settles the economy per-PLANET precisely on *"you cannot compete for a mine that only exists inside your own instance"*.  ⚠ **Compact is the requirement rather than an optimisation**: `@X243` makes a server a persistent store and not a live host, so what it keeps is a few rows per sortie, for ever.  ⚠⚠ **And the world half must DECAY** — `DESIGN.md` § 20's *nothing may permanently close* and `@X228`'s *grow and crumble needs decay* — so a collapsed POI is being rebuilt between sorties, which is `@X304`'s swarm answering a broken thing ONE SYSTEM UP, on a longer clock.  A raid is a setback inflicted, not a hole punched, and a half-repaired mine is the most legible thing a persistent world can show.  ⚠ It is `ROADMAP.md` item **7 — carryover**, with item **6 — the landing flow** as its other end, and it makes `ROBOT_ECONOMY.md` § Open questions 1's per-planet answer concrete: *cutting a route in one sortie changes the next one*.  ⚠ **OPEN: the rate of decay** — too fast and a raid buys nothing, too slow and a planet only accumulates damage | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `plans/28`, `@X298`, `@X304`, `@X177`, `@X188`, `@X243`, `@X228` |
| `@X307` | ⚠⚠ **THE COARSE MAP IS A REAL MAP, AT 1.5 km A HEX, MODELLED ON `../crawler`'s ORTLER REGION** (owner, 2026-08-28: *"there will be a world map in 1.5 km eventually like the ortler map in ../crawler, with routines to determine details from that"*).  ⚠ So the server's state is not a table of rates hanging in space — it is a MAP with the economy laid onto it, and the reference implementation is `crawler`'s `src/ortlergen.loft` / `src/overland.loft` / `src/realworld/` / `data/regions/` / `plans/1-ortler-worldgen-fixture/`.  ⚠⚠ **Which means a scenario's TERRAIN is derived and not only its economy**, and `@X298`'s ratio is the size of the job: **one coarse hex has to produce a million fine ones**.  ⚠ **dryopea has NO procedural generation of any kind today** — the three maps in `maps/` are authored `.keys` built into committed `.json` pairs and there is no seed anywhere in `src/` — so this is a first, and two written rules govern it.  ⚠⚠ **`@X224`: other players are the SEED**, because a seed the author chose is a world the author knows.  ⚠⚠ **And the GATES need determinism, which is not a conflict**: a seed is unknowable in advance but not UN-RECORDED — once a cell is generated its seed is a concrete value, and `plans/18` already turns any reached state into a `.keys` fixture, so the gates test *a* world exactly while live worlds come from history nobody can precompute.  ⚠ It closes a loop with `@X306`: **the delta a sortie hands back IS the player history `@X224` wants**, so the two rulings are one mechanism read from opposite ends | `docs/ERRANDS.md`, `@X224`, `@X298`, `@X306`, `plans/18` |
| `@X308` | ⚠⚠ **A PLANET HAS TWO LAYERS — THE GIVEN AND THE ACCUMULATED — AND A REAL-WORLD MAP IS AN AUTHOR-FREE SEED** (owner, 2026-08-28: *"this will be populated by user actions, yes.
